The sixteen arms are the portions of the moon; know that these are likewise of sixteen aspects. By blocking the eight openings he is the supreme lord with eight faces. The moon, the sun, darkness, and fire are blocked by his feet. Having blocked the half-moons, with the twenty-four arms and the four faces, as before, the Wheel of Time was fully manifested. By blocking the consonants, ka and the rest, there is the other with thirty-four arms. By blocking the sixteen vowels, the sixteen feet are taught. By blocking the nine doors, Vajrabhairava has nine faces. Such tantras and the like come from the blocking of body, speech, and mind. These have been taught by the Lord of the Victors to the childish, from the ground onward.

So it says. In the root tantra it is also taught at length; I have explained only a little for the sake of the understanding of the childish.

The full practice of the root of this very chapter on the families should be understood as the commentary on the meaning condensed in the thirteen and a half verses of the Cakrasaṃvara Laghu. This is the certainty of the Tathāgatas; one tantra is to be understood through another tantra. Thus, with thirty verses, the certainty of the root tantra was spoken by the Blessed One in the chapter on the Hevajra family. In the six-thousand-verse commentary on Hevajra, this is the fifth chapter, on the family, which gathers the yoga of inner heat and is the cause of generation. The chapter on the family is complete.

Having shown only the meaning of the various topics, and having bowed with my head to the Enjoyment Body, in this vehicle I shall explain here, through symbolic expression, this very secret mantra, and the meaning of the families and the rest.

The symbolic expression of the mantras of the buddhas is made clear in the tantra in two ways. One is the symbol of suchness; the second is the declaration of conventional usage. What is called the symbol of suchness is the language of all sentient beings, the words of speech: the conch of the Dharma, endowed with a great sound, the great gong of the Dharma. The one who does not abide, who has passed beyond sorrow, is said to speak in speech that goes everywhere. The mantra of conventional declaration is the one that makes the meaning clear through symbolic expression.

The recitation that follows the meaning is the mantra without division of parts, beautiful through lacking intrinsic nature. One abides reciting in the state of the neuter. The life-breath flows without break, and joy increases. One recites, having arrived at the place of the avadhūtī. It is twenty-one thousand six hundred in a day and a night. In five days it is a hundred thousand, with eight thousand more.

Beginning from the first of the dark fortnight, one continues until three full moons have passed. Beginning from the first of the bright fortnight, one recites until three completions. At the time of the dawn's rising, on the full moon, it is accomplished. Having bowed to Vajrasattva, one turns the wheel of Dharma well. With eight thousand recitations, and likewise forty thousand, and with six hundred thousand, one month brings about the accomplishment of the recitation of the life-breath. It is not by a rosary, and not by speaking aloud.

Fire offering, worship with mantra, satisfying offerings, and other rites, one after another, are all thoroughly destroyed. Those mantrins of childish minds who are powerless in this recitation: for them the terminology of the counting rosary is explained, for those who desire outward enjoyment, so that the mantrin may obtain merit.

By day and by night, let the life-breath enter the central channel. Having entered, it carries off diseases. If the life-breath is bound for the days of the lunar date, this bestows the state of moving in the sky. If for one month the life-breath is purified and abides in the heart, yogins become deathless. If the navel is burned for six months, this bestows omniscience itself.

The wheels at the forehead, the throat, the heart and the navel, in due order, bring unconsciousness, binding, killing, and burning, as appropriate to each. When the life-breath and the downward-clearing wind are stopped, freedom from desire is what is wished for. By the two, killing and burning, the mantra practitioner becomes a buddha. When the life-breath and the downward-clearing wind are exhausted, the twelve links come to be exhausted here. By the stopping of cause and effect, who would not become a buddha?

As long as the life-breaths of living beings move, so long does rebirth continue. When the movements of the life-breath have perished, there is no death and no further rebirth.

Thus the recitation of the mantra's coded signs is that of the neuter. It is to be understood through the code of suchness.

Here the Blessed One said: "From this point on, the chapter on mantras will be explained." This is the torma mantra of all beings. "All" and "beings" together mean all the bhūtas, those who have come into being. Their mantras are to be taken in the number that applies in each case.

Oṃ akāro mukhaṃ sarvadharmāṇāṃ, adyanutpannatvāt.

Here the sound of the letter A is the sound of the indestructible. By such lines as "The letter A is the supreme of all letters, the great, unchanging, sacred meaning; the great life-breath is without birth, free of being expressed in words," one should understand the net of illusion. "All" means the omniscient, as in such lines as "The omniscient, the all-knowing, the sacred one, who knows himself and knows others, all of it; the supreme person who benefits all, who has passed beyond worldly comparison, the supreme lord of knowing and the knowable." The mantra of omniscience is "the speech of all beings in their own languages," and this is the definitive meaning.

Those born from the elements such as earth are the bhūtas, beings whose nature is the five aggregates. Their mantra is the five syllables whose nature is the five aggregates. The syllable Oṃ is their aggregate of form, the vajra body. The syllable Āḥ is the aggregate of perception, the vajra speech. The syllable Hūṃ is the aggregate of consciousness, the vajra mind. The syllable Svā is the aggregate of feeling. The syllable Hā is the aggregate of formations. The torma is offered to them, and at the end it is sent off with three Hūṃ syllables and two Phaṭ syllables.

Thus the syllable Oṃ purifies. Āḥ increases. Hūṃ makes it blaze. Svā makes it nectar. Hā summons. The three Hūṃ offer. The two Phaṭ send off.

With the left hand one purifies. With the right hand one increases. Covering with the joined palms, one makes it blaze. With the gesture of the garuḍa one makes it nectar. With the gesture of Trailokyavijaya one summons. With the vajra gesture one offers. With the hand gesture, joining the thumbs and fingers, one requests departure. The binding of these gestures should be understood from other tantras.

Likewise, the seed syllables of the tathāgatas, in the numbers that apply, are as follows. The syllable Bhrūṃ is Vairocana, at the crown. The syllable Āṃ is Ratnasambhava, at the eyes. The syllable Jrīṃ is Amitābha, at the throat. The syllable Khaṃ is Amoghasiddhi, at the secret place. The syllable Hūṃ is Akṣobhya, at the heart. Having thus placed these on the body of Piṭu-vajra, one does the same for the other vajra ḍākinīs, and this is the definitive meaning.

Then the mantra of the sixteen-armed one is: Oṃ deva piṭu vajra hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. Here the definitive meaning is this. The syllable Oṃ is the seat of the deities, and so, by the first syllable of the mantra, the deity is accomplished. The remaining seed syllables place the limbs, in due order, at the eyes and the other places. With the three Hūṃ the wisdom being is summoned. With Phaṭ it is made to enter, with Svā it is delighted, and with Hā it is made of one taste. This is the heart essence of Hevajra.

Thus all mantras begin with the syllable Oṃ, end with Svāhā, and have Hūṃ and Phaṭ added in the middle. This holds in every case.

In the same way as before, the mantra of the six-armed one is: Oṃ kiṭi kiṭi vajra hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. Likewise that of the four-armed one is: Oṃ jvala jvala bhyo hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. Likewise that of the two-armed one is: Oṃ trailokyākṣepa hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. As for Hūṃ and Phaṭ in the chapter on the ten mantras, these are to be learned from the oral instruction.

The mantra that blesses the body, speech and mind of all is this. The syllable Oṃ blesses the body, Vairocana, on the disc of the moon. The syllable Āḥ blesses the speech, Amitābha, on the sun. The syllable Hūṃ blesses the mind, Akṣobhya, at the heart on the disc of Rāhu.

For the sake of guiding the provisional meaning, the vowels beginning with Nairātmyā are to be recited for the purpose of the ten mantras: a ā, i ī, u ū, ṛ ṝ, ḷ ḹ, e ai, o au, aṃ aḥ. According to the definitive meaning as well, they are to be grasped by the recitation that gathers them separately: a i ṛ u ḷ ka, a e ar o al ca, ha ya ra va la ṭa sa. These seed syllables belong to the portions of the moon in the bright fortnight. That this was not spoken by the Blessed One in the manner of children's reading, as a provisional meaning, is the definitive meaning established in the root tantra.

Likewise the mantra that shakes the city is: Oṃ a ka ca ṭa ta pa ya śa svāhā. This is by the provisional meaning. By the definitive meaning, a, ka, ku, ha, which are to be analyzed, become the throat. I, ca, ya, śa become the palate. Ṛ, ṭa, ra, ṣa become the crown. U, pa, va, and the labials become the lips. Ḷ, ta, la, sa become the teeth.

Oṃ a ā ha hā ṅa ña ṇa ma na ṅka svāhā is the mantra that stirs up the city. The chapter on the ten mantras is to be learned from the guru's speech. According to the definitive meaning in the root tantra, the oral instruction is that the thirty moments are distinguished by the portions of the moon, the vowels and consonants.

Day by day, for each day, the sixty vowels and consonants of the sun should be recited, with the divisions of the staff. By the sounds a, ka, ca, ṭa, ta, pa, ya, śa, the vowel-and-consonant series (āli and kāli) is spoken in its definitive meaning. It is said that if one recites the āli and kāli a hundred thousand times, one shakes the three worlds, so what need is there to speak of a single city.

The āli and kāli should be spoken, namely: a ā i ī ṛ ṝ u ū ḷ ḹ e ai ar ār o au al āl ha hā ya yā ra rā va vā la lā. In this expanding sequence the breath enters with the recitation of the āli. When the breath goes out, there is the kāli: ka kā kha khā ga gā gha ghā ṅa ṅā; ca cā cha chā ja jā jha jhā ña ñā; ṭa ṭā ṭha ṭhā ḍa ḍā ḍha ḍhā ṇa ṇā; pa pā pha phā ba bā bha bhā ma mā; ta tā tha thā da dā dha dhā na nā; sa sā va vā ṣa ṣā śa śā, and the final syllables.

At the beginning one generates the syllable oṃ. For summoning, one adds voṣaṭ at the end. Such is the mantra for shaking a city. It is to be spoken for the meditation on a single deity, with recitation over red flowers, and with a fire offering, as will be explained.

In the same way one should speak the mantra of Kurukullā. Here the syllable oṃ accomplishes the body support. The syllable hrīḥ completes the speech of the goddess. In the wind family, it is sealed by Kurukullā with Amitābha. Oṃ is at the forehead; hrīḥ at the tongue; ku at the throat; ru at the heart; ku at the navel; and lle at the secret place. This is the placing of the limbs. Having filled oneself with the in-breath, one recites a hundred thousand times, and at the time of the out-breath one should not move the rosary. This is the mantra of Kurukullā.

Now the definitive meaning of the protective mantra will be explained. The syllable oṃ speaks of the gentle seat, also a pleasing place, moon-like, with fragrant ground. The syllable ra is the sun disc of the seven suns. The syllable kṣa is the crossed vajra. Below it, above it, and in all directions one makes a wheel of ten spokes. Then in the center, from the syllable hūṃ, arises the lord of the wrathful kings. On the eastern spoke is Yamāntaka, the Ender. On the southern spoke is Prajñāntaka. On the western spoke is Padmāntaka. On the northern spoke is Vighnāntaka. In the fire direction is Acala. In the direction of the southwest is Ṭakkirāja, king of desire. In the corner of the wind is Nīladaṇḍa. In the northeast corner is Mahābala. On the upper spoke is Uṣṇīṣa. Below is Sumbharāja. Then, by speaking the second, one has the sun disc of the hoped-for. By the syllable kṣa, the crossed vajra, all becomes one, and so there is the triple-excellent vajra. Through the three syllables hūṃ there are the vajra ground, the tiered mansion, and the vajra lotus seat.

The syllable phaṭ is for striking the malevolent with the dagger. The syllable svā is for invoking the buddhas and bodhisattvas. By the syllable hā, all the offerings and so forth, and the confession of misdeeds and so forth, will be declared. This is the definitive meaning of this mantra.

Now there will be those who accomplish the twenty-four activities taught in the root tantra, and the words of the mantras should be spoken. Here, in the abbreviated tantra, everything that accords with the procedure and everything that reverses it is concealed from teachers who are wicked and do not fully understand. It should be known through the sequence of the lineage of the holy lama and so forth, not through reading the books. Otherwise, one should consult other tantras and grasp whatever words of the Blessed One are reasonable, and are correctly realized across many tantras, not through the mere faith of a fool.

Thus the Blessed One said: "My word is virtuous in the beginning, virtuous in the middle, and virtuous in the end. It is not virtuous at the beginning and non-virtuous in the middle and end, nor non-virtuous in the middle and virtuous at the end." As foolish people imagine: at the beginning meditating on conceptual thought, in the middle meditating on the bliss of the two faculties, and at the end meditating on non-conceptual emptiness, meditating on great bliss. That is like a bad cause of words, and every fruit born from it is seen to be of that kind.

From the seed of the kodrava,
no rice grain ever comes.
From the seed of conceptual thought
the fruit becomes conceptual.
What is born from non-conceptuality
becomes a fruit that is non-conceptual.
For this reason it is virtuous at the beginning,
virtuous in the middle, and virtuous at the end.

As the seed, so the sprout;
as the tree, so the fruit.
This is seen throughout the world,
and this is dependent origination itself.
Whatever mantra belongs to another family
does not accomplish another action.
Nowhere in the world
does the sun pacify heat.

As taught clearly to me
in the root tantra of Hevajra,
just so in this abbreviated tantra
I shall give the extensive explanation.

By the seed of earth: paralyzing,
bewildering, and striking with the dagger.
By the seed of water: always pacifying,
likewise increasing and neutralizing poison.
By the seed of fire: strongly summoning,
always empowering and moving.
By the seeds of wind: causing hatred, expelling,
and likewise increasing poison.
By the seed of space: removing poison,
likewise killing and harming.
Nourishing, inciting, and delighting
are accomplished by the seed of wisdom.

Muteness, deafness, and making a person impotent are likewise accomplished by the mantrin, in due order, through the seed syllables of mind, speech, and body. From the union of earth, water, fire, wind, and space with the maṇḍala of wisdom, the rites of paralysis and the like are accomplished by the supreme practitioner. Making impotent, making mute, blindness, blocking the nose, deafness, and madness are accomplished with the seed syllables of the former kind.

These rites are to be performed in mountains, in mountain wilds, in medicinal meadows, on the shores of great oceans, and in charnel grounds that have existed from the beginning. Mantra recitation, meditation, fire offering, and the raising of the maṇḍala should accord with each particular rite; otherwise no accomplishment will be granted. In another place, neither men nor women accomplish mantra and deity. Once the deity is urged on by the mantra, it comes to perform the rites. Therefore the wise should recite and meditate on the name and on the body of the deity. The root mantra becomes the body. The one called the heart mantra is spoken as powerful. The near-heart mantra is called the mantra of mind. What is taught as the wisdom seed is to be called the wisdom mantra. Seed syllable, near-heart mantra, heart mantra, and root mantra correspond to wisdom, mind, speech, and body; in this order they are to be meditated upon and recited. Until accomplishment arises, recite and offer with diligence. Otherwise, when the ritual procedure is spoiled, no accomplishment of mantra will arise. For one who grasps with faith alone, the body is nothing but affliction.

The syllables ya, ra, la, and va arise from wind, fire, earth, and water, and are to be applied without fail to the east, south, west, and north. In the sequence of long Ī, fire, Nairṛti, wind, and Īśāna, give haṃ and ha above and below, and in the center make offering with aṃ and aḥ. For the sake of reading the buddhas, the lamas, and the sacred Dharma, one makes a maṇḍala in front.

The other, the Victor, the sovereign himself, arises through the maṇḍala of wind: round, the east, the body of Videha. By the nature of the maṇḍala of fire: Jambudvīpa, three-cornered. Four-cornered is the west, Godānīya. The maṇḍala of earth has its own defining mark. By the maṇḍala of water, the half-moon, the north, Kuru, of unpleasing sound. By the nature of emptiness, the maṇḍala of the moon has the defining mark of the Brahmā gate. Below, by the nature of wisdom, is the maṇḍala of the sun, the fire of time. In the center, a distinct drop becomes the mountain of the gods, above and below. Having made the maṇḍala in this way, one then recites the mantras and so forth. Any other maṇḍala on the ground is to be made like a colored-powder maṇḍala.

One constantly makes offering with the thirty-six: the aggregates, the six elements, form, and so on. Earth, water, fire, wind, semen, and blood-sediment are spoken of as the six elements. The aggregate of form belongs to embodied beings. It becomes the throat, the palate, the crown of the head, the lips, the teeth, and the nose. The aggregate of perception is taught as the six kinds that illuminate the meaning of things existent and non-existent. Eye, ear, nose, tongue, body, and likewise the mind faculty are explained as the aggregate of consciousness; the six, eye and the rest, are of that kind. Likewise form, sound, smells, tastes, touch, and the dharmadhātu are explained as feelings, because they are experienced. In the same way the consciousnesses enter through the doors of the six faculties, and the objects and faculties bring about the entering. The aggregate of formations is taught as sixfold: the life-breath and the downward-clearing wind, and the three paths, upper and lower, flowing in the six channels. The aggregate of wisdom is also sixfold. The thirty-seventh is the great mind.

The yogin should make offering with a, the vowels, and ka, the consonants, to the cycle of the sacred thirty-six elements. By the nature of the vowels and the consonants, the elements are seventy-two. Of these, the six are the syllables ha and the rest. Through the division into short and long, the seventy-two are taught here. The vowels are thirty-six, joined with the vowels and the consonants. One hundred and eight syllables are to be spoken on the rosary. The yogin recites the mantra. Of the thirty-six consonants, with the short vowels, eighteen, they are joined with the vowels; with one half of a hundred and eight, they are to be spoken there on the rosary. By division of the lunar mansions into quarters, the third is to be spoken in full. By a half portion of the lunar mansions, the fourth is that very thing. By dividing the thirty-six vowels, the fifth is to be spoken in full. By dividing the enjoyment of the lunar mansions, the sixth is to be spoken in full. By dividing the eighteen vowels, the seventh is to be spoken in full. Again, by dividing the thirty-six consonants, there is the eighth. By the rosary of the ascetic, it is explained in this way as eightfold.

With eighteen crystal beads, one performs a single pacifying rite, by one of firm vows. By reciting the thirty-six vowels with pearls, human beings are increased. The twenty-seven lunar mansions are taught for killing one who cannot be revived. Its variant, with the revival of a donkey, is for causing hatred and for banishing. With the thirty-six pure consonants, bringing under control and the rest is done with a pothi leaf. Mixing vowels and consonants completely, with fifty-four, one calls it the lotus, or else, with the sandalwood seed, one summons. With a quarter of the lunar mansions, one causes bewilderment; the lungthang is taught. With the vowels and consonants in quarters, for paralysis it is taught with a rudrākṣa. Thus, for the pure rite, pure kinds are taught in eight ways. For mixed rites of various kinds, one works with the seed syllable or here with a jewel. Do as one finds fitting according to the division of the colors of the elements. Then from this, whatever brings the yogin quick accomplishment.

The mantra that has arisen from the family: the defining marks of mantra will be explained. The a, ku, ha, and ka that arise from the throat, the vowels and the consonants and their defining marks, arise from the element of space; they are known by the syllable ha. The i, ca, ya, and śa arise from the palate; the vowels and consonants and their defining marks arise from the element of wind; they are known by the syllable ya. The ṛ, ṭa, ra, and ṣa arise from the crown of the head; the vowels and consonants and their defining marks arise from the element of fire; this is by the syllable ra.

Those that arise from the upper lip, the syllables U and Pu, Va and the labial sounds, whichever they are, these vowels and consonants and their defining marks arise truly from the water element. They are known through the syllable Va. Those born from the teeth and the syllables Lṛ, Tu, La, Sa, these vowels and consonants and their defining marks arise truly from the earth element. They are known through the syllable La. The visarga and the anusvāra, though they lack the vowel and consonant, arise truly from the element of wisdom. Thus there are the families of the six elements: earth, water, fire (the devourer), wind, seminal fluid, and the family of dust (menstrual blood). The six elements are explained here as the great person, the great being.

The chief ones, the vowels beginning with A and the consonants beginning with Ka, are the lords of the aggregates and elements, the very lords of mantra. The gathering together of each one is taught for the sake of establishing the sound that brings bliss. Mantra becoming accomplished is just this; so too the mantra of omniscience. It is not by the order of a child's reading. The vowels and consonants that are mixed, produced from throat and palate or from throat and lips, those that come from two places, are not born of a single nature. The vowels born of pure nature arise in a single place, such as the throat. When mantras are always pronounced, that is not pronounced from two places. The syllable A, fully joined with a vowel, does not leave its own place; that is pure by nature. A consonant with the visarga and the dot, joined with vowels such as E, that enters two places is not born of pure nature, and the consonant becomes doubled.

The great mighty maṇḍala is yellow, arising from the syllable Laṃ. The water maṇḍala is white, arising from the syllable Vaṃ. The fire maṇḍala is red, arising from the syllable Raṃ. The wind maṇḍala is black, arising from the syllable Yaṃ. The space maṇḍala is green, arising from the syllable Haṃ. What arises from the visarga and the dot is born from all the colors of wisdom. Likewise the Ta class and the syllable Lṛ, born of that letter, are yellow. The Pa class and the others, with the syllable U, born of the vowel, are said to be white. The Ṭa class, with the syllable Ṛ, born of the vowel, are red. The Ca class, with the syllable I, born of the vowel, are said to be black. The Ka class and the others, with the syllable A, born of the vowel, are said to be green.

Likewise, those born from the syllable Sa, and from A and La, are yellow. White are those born from the syllable Va, and the syllable O born of its quality. Red are those born from the syllable Ṣa, and the syllable Ar born of its quality. Black are those born from the syllable Śa, and the syllable E born of its quality. Green are those born from the syllable Ha, and the syllable Ai born of its quality. These, A, I, Ṛ, U, Lṛ, are the vowels born of pure nature. These, A, E, Ar, O, Al, are born of the two, quality and nature. These, Ha, Ya, Ra, Va, La, are likewise the two converted by the semivowel change. The ordinary ones with the chief one stand in the middle of the two; the chief leads the maṇḍala.

Again, within each of the classes, the nature is explained as fivefold. These, Ṅa, Ña, Ṇa, Ma, Na, arise from the element of space. These, Gha, Jha, Ḍha, Bha, Dha, arise truly from the element of wind. These, Ga, Ja, Ḍa, Ba, Da, arise truly from the element of fire. These, Kha, Cha, Ṭha, Pha, Tha, arise from the element of water. These, Ka, Ca, Ṭa, Pa, Ta, arise from the element of earth. By the division of the six families of letters, and by the division of the chief natures, through the division of each separate letter, the families become fivefold.

As earth and its tail are yellow, so are form, the body and its faculty. As water and the moon are white, so are perception and the tongue faculty. As fire and the sun are red, so are feeling and the eye, these too. As wind and Rāhu are black, so are formations and the nose. As space and the pole star are green, so are consciousness and the ear. As the hare-marked moon is blue, so wisdom, and likewise the mind is said to be wisdom. In the same way smell, color, taste, tangible objects, sound, and also the dharmadhātu are the six families of elements, in order.

Likewise the anus, the feet, the hands, the faculty of speech, and the supreme faculty of the bhaga, all arise from the families of earth and the rest. Likewise speaking, taking, going, excreting, urinating, and emitting seed, these actions arise from the families of earth and the rest. Thus the six elements are fully declared: the six aggregates, the six faculties, the objects, and the faculties of action, and the six functions of the faculties of action. By dividing the six aggregates and the elements, the elements number thirty-six; the thirty-seventh is wisdom, the oneness of the emptiness of all phenomena. The elements are in the outer world and also in the body; as in the body, so in others.

Pure earth is for immobilizing. Pure water is for pacifying. Pure fire is for bringing under control. Pure wind is for causing hatred. Pure space is for sustaining life. Pure wisdom is for killing. These are called the six pure rites.

The others are the mixed ones. By the mixing of earth and water, all embodied beings flourish. By the mixing of wind and fire, all embodied beings are drawn in. By the mixing of wind and water, people are set in motion. By the mixing of earth and space, embodied beings fall. By the mixing of earth and wisdom, the fallen are raised up again. By the mixing of fire and water, men and women are dissolved in desire. By the mixing of earth and wind, there is bewilderment and being pierced with the ritual dagger. By the mixing of space and water, beings are made to tremble. By the mixing of space and wisdom, there is life once more. By the mixing of fire and earth, all embodied beings are tormented. By the mixing of fire and space, embodied beings fall ill with disease. By the mixing of fire and wisdom, embodied beings are thrown into turmoil. By the mixing of wind and space, the various ones are surely driven out. By the mixing of wind and wisdom, in an instant the three realms are bound. By the mixing of space and wisdom, all embodied beings are shaken.

By wisdom alone, all rites are accomplished.
Those who speak of wisdom perform them.
Not by recitation, nor by counting a rosary,
nor by fire offering, mantra, or offerings,
but by meditative concentration alone
does the yogin bring all rites to success.

Thus, whatever mantras belong to the three families,
to the four, or to the five families,
all arise from the nature of the elements,
and by rite they perform endless activities.
In this way the mantras of the six families
are pure and also mixed with one another.
These are the great beings without number.
To accomplish the divine siddhis,
the characteristics of the mantras are to be told
according to the nature of the five elements,
for the sake of beings, with respect to enemy, friend, and neutral.

A, Ku, Ha, Ka, and those arising from the throat,
the vowels and consonants with their characteristics,
are always present as friends
with the mantras of space, wind, and the rest.

I, Tsu, Ya, Śa, and those arising from the palate,
the vowels and consonants with their characteristics,
arise truly from the element of wind;
they are the enemy of those born from water.

Ṛ, Ṭu, Ra, Ṣa, born from the crown of the head,
the vowels and consonants with their characteristics,
arise truly from the element of fire;
they are the enemy of those born from earth.

U, Pu, Va, Pa, born from the lips,
the vowels and consonants with their characteristics,
arise truly from the element of water;
they are the enemy of those born from fire.

Ḷ, Tu, La, Sa, born from the teeth,
the vowels and consonants with their characteristics,
arise truly from the element of earth;
they are the enemy of those born from wind.

Space is always the friend of wind.
Neutral is water, because it lacks power.
Fire's friend is always wind.
Earth is neutral, because it lacks power.
Water's friend is earth, and fire is the neutral one.
Earth's friend is water, and fire too is neutral.

Setting aside the syllable Oṃ,
the first letter of the mantra is the family;
that itself is the heart of the mantra,
and the form is the cause of completion.
When other consonant letters are stacked
upon the first letter of the mantra,
of these the first is the one taken,
because it is spoken first.
By the distinction of vowel and consonant,
these become two kinds.
The enemy and friend of the life force
and of the body are also to be told.
The enemy, friend, or neutral of the life force
are taught through the vowels;
the enemy, friend, and neutral of the body
are taught through the consonants.
Through the enemy among the vowels,
the life of the practitioner is taken, without doubt.
Through one characterized by an enemy among the consonants,
illness and the like are brought about.
Within a single class, too, Ka and the rest
should be known in relation to the practitioner's mantra.
Ṅa, Ña, Ṇa, Na, Ma: these,
and the others, remain as friends.
Gha, Jha, Ḍha, Bha, Dha: these
are the enemy of those born from water.
Ga, Ja, Ḍa, Ba, Da: these
are the enemy of those born from earth.
Kha, Cha, Ṭha, Pha, Tha: these
are the enemy of those born from fire.
Ka, Ca, Ṭa, Pa, Ta: these
are the enemy of those born from wind.
Thus friend and neutral are to be known
mutually, as before.

The first letter of the mantra stands,
whether in its own class or in others,
and the practitioner's two letters
become the one born from birth and the one born from the name.
These too are to be fully known as two kinds,
having the characteristics of vowel and consonant.
At the time of birth, the conjunction of the time
is of the nature of the five vowel signs.
For a man or a woman, whether born under Capricorn or another sign,
the consonant of that sign should be purified.
For Capricorn, the five from Ka onward
should be purified by the distinction of the vowel signs.
For Aquarius too, the five from Ṅa onward,
distinguished in order by empty and the rest.
For Pisces, the five from Tsa onward;
for Aries, the five from Ña in order;
for Taurus, the five from Ṭa onward;
for Gemini, the five from Ṇa likewise;
for the conjunction of Cancer, Pa and the rest;
for the conjunction of Leo, Ma and the rest.
For Virgo, the five from Ta onward;
for Libra likewise, the five from Na onward;
for Scorpio, the five from Sa onward;
for Sagittarius, the five from Tha onward are taught.

By the distinction of withdrawal and emission,
sixty letters are assigned to day and night.
For the movement of the breath to right and left,
the five maṇḍalas descend,
and by their division into six periods,
the outer world, and likewise the body, are determined.
The vowels are to be purified in the middle of the lunar portions,
by the distinction of thirty.
Beginning from the first day of the waxing fortnight,
up to the full moon, there are thirty deities.
A, I, Ṛ, U, Ḷ: these
are assigned in order to the first day and onward.
A, E, Ar, O, Al: these
are well known as the sixth day and onward.
Ha, Ya, Ra, Va, La: these
are taught for the eleventh day and onward.
The five beginning with La, in reverse order,
belong to the first day of the dark fortnight and onward.
The five beginning with the letter A
belong to the sixth day of the dark fortnight and onward.
The five beginning with the letter Ḷ,
among the vowels, are for the eleventh day and onward.
At the full moon the dot is spoken;
at the end of the new moon the visarga is placed.
By the finer division of moments,
these are taught as the portions of each moon.
The vowel that rises in any given portion
is the one that chiefly governs beings.
The five consonants that have properties,
these belong to the families of earth and the rest.
In the first moment the vowel is such,
and it becomes the vowel born from it;
in the second, from the second,
and thus all in order.
By the distinction of the thirty moments,
the born vowel should be spoken.

By the first sound of the mantra,
through vowels and consonants without doubt,
practitioners will exhaust their life
or suffer great illness, there is no doubt.
When the vowel is dull, one does not know it,
and when the consonant is free of conceptuality,
the first vowel of the name should be taken,
and also the first of the two consonants.
Whether the first consonant of the mantra or the vowels,
for the purpose of accomplishing them
one performs the approach recitation.
Here six rites are taught.
The first is striking;
likewise bringing down, and burning;
then drawing out by mantra,
and then increase and pleasing.

The emptiness made with the visarga
presses upon the front of the mantra;
this is the striking by the king of weapons,
and one comes to a state of fainting.
By a hundred thousand recitations of the mantra,
the deity of the mantra is made victorious.
Pride is wholly abandoned,
and the practitioner comes under one's power.
Likewise, pressed down by wind,
it descends upon the yogin.
Pressed down by fire, it burns.
Then by water it is drawn out.
Earth, abiding at the crown,
makes the deity increase through recitation.
Pressed down on the head with the bindu and its accessories,
the one pleased grants the supreme.
Thus by six hundred thousand recitations
the preliminary approach is taught,
and in all the root tantras the Sugata taught it
for the sake of the desired siddhis.

The syllable phaṭ, likewise hūṃ, bauṣaṭ, namaḥ, svāhā, and vaṣaṭ: these are used for the six activities, according to their number. The practitioner who keeps the observance should place them at the end of the mantra. First he should give the offering to Vairocana, and then begin the recitation again. After that he should recite the divisions, and perform the fire offering with one tenth of the recitation. He should do everything according to the rites taught in the tantra. Then the deity will be accomplished, and, being accomplished, will bestow on the mantrin the supreme attainment, whatever he seeks.

Rites of other families, other kinds of activity, other times, other mantra families, other places, directions, and supports: none of these activities bears fruit. Those who recite mantras read from books, and who have abandoned the oral instruction, kindle a fire of accomplishment that is itself an affliction for the ground. If one finds them in a book, what use is it to have taken them up correctly? Whatever is written down and read out has only been clarified as a provisional meaning. Those who have abandoned the true substance of the sacred mantra and recite from books mantras for gods and the like: such practitioners wish to eat the sky. But a practitioner who has steadfast effort in his own mind, or who accomplishes mantra recitation while seeking the good of others, by his wish attains worldly accomplishments. If mantra recitation, fire offering, the place, and the sequence of the offering rite are impaired, the rite will not succeed. This statement is true and just so. For it is shown by the Teacher, by bodhisattvas, and by the gods; therefore the wise should take up this very point with every effort.

With these and similar words, the root tantra taught the accomplishment of mantras by other methods, for the sake of accomplishing activities such as paralyzing. In this short tantra, however, the meaning is to be understood through another, intentional language, and that is what will be explained. "Oṃ hūṃ svāhā: it paralyzes." By the syllable hūṃ one should accomplish the vajra earth element, and by it one should accomplish paralyzing. "Oṃ aṃ svāhā: it brings under control." By the syllable aṃ one should accomplish the fire element, and by it one brings under control. "Oṃ khaṃ svāhā: it expels." By the syllable khaṃ one should accomplish the wind element, and by it one expels. "Oṃ jrīṃ svāhā: it sows hatred." By the syllable jrīṃ one should accomplish the elements of fire and wind mixed, and by them one causes hatred. "Oṃ bhrūṃ svāhā: it is a fierce rite." By the syllable bhrūṃ one should accomplish fire and water, and by them one performs the fierce rite. "Oṃ hūṃ svāhā: it summons." By the syllable hūṃ one should accomplish the elements of wind, fire, and space, and by them one summons. "Oṃ ghuḥ svāhā: it kills." By the syllable ghuḥ one should accomplish wind and water, and by them one kills. This was spoken with intention.

What is the direct perception of the faculties? It is the direct perception imputed by the mind. The wisdom of the generation stage is not the direct perception that makes a buddha. What is the direct perception of the yogī? It is that which is self-awareness. The wisdom of the completion stage is the supreme that accomplishes the work of a buddha.

That same wisdom has five distinct aspects. At the beginning, the middle, and the end, the five eyes were taught by the Protector in gathering the teachings. First the eye of flesh, then the divine eye, then the eye of a buddha. After that comes the eye of wisdom (prajñā), and then the fifth, the eye of gnosis (jñāna). For the beginner yogī, the first becomes the eye of flesh. Then through it comes the divine eye, and one sees as the gods see. After that it becomes the eye of a buddha, and one sees as the śrāvakas see. Then, as with the eye of wisdom, one sees as the bodhisattvas see. Then comes the eye of the nature of gnosis, and one sees as the Sugatas see. Whoever possesses the eye of the four kinds of gnosis is called by that name. By the distinction of gnosis and consciousness, two kinds of entities are shown: for those who are still training, the eye of gnosis, and after that what is called the eye of gnosis proper. A buddha has no beginning and no end. The first buddha is the two without conceptual thought, the single eye of gnosis, free of stain. The Tathāgata is the one who has gnosis as his body.

Thus, in the conventional truth of the world, according to the way of dependent origination, "when this is obtained, this arises." In the combination of orpiment and ash, as with the red itself, this should be understood for accomplishing all activities. This is not done by yogīs with the pride of thinking "I am a buddha." From this comes the certainty of accomplishment.

This is the sixth chapter, the section on the accomplishment of the limitless, from the chapter on the mantra families in the six-thousand-verse commentary on the Hevajra.

Having bowed to the hūṃ of Hevajra, I will explain the various accomplishments, out of compassion for sentient beings, in order to bring down rain upon the earth.

"Having made an image of Ananta with the two syllables a and phuḥ": here the image of Ananta should be made from fragrant sandalwood. From the navel up it has the form of a man, and below it has the form of a serpent's tail. It has four arms: in the left, a skull cup of nectar and a lotus; in the right, a fruit of nectar and a jewel. Over its head it has seven hoods in the form of a canopy. Likewise, to its left, the nāga king is made by the syllable aṃ. One should wash it with the five nectars: milk, curd, honey, butter, and sugar are the five nectars, and with these it should be washed. Then one should make offerings with blue utpala flowers. One should anoint the body with the fluid that subdues nāgas, and anoint the head with elephant liquor. Joining two cups mouth to mouth, one fills them with the milk of a black cow. With four fingers one places a measure of twelve finger-widths and a half, reaching from the forehead and the nose to the end of the lips. One should bind it in the eight directions with a thread spun by a young black girl and treated by degrees. Then, at the edge of a pond, on the windward side, one makes a maṇḍala, and in the middle of the pond one places Ananta, the nāga king. In the colored-powder maṇḍala one draws, with colored powders, Hevajra suppressing Ananta. Here I will explain the coloring of the maṇḍala: the black powder is made from charcoal of a charnel ground.

With powder of human bones, white; with cow bile, yellow; with the tusk of a boar from a charnel ground, red; and with thief's leaves mixed with a little human bone, green. With these five colored powders one raises the maṇḍala. First one lays out the lines with a charnel-ground thread, that is, a thread made from the sinews of corpses. Using measures of three cubits and a little more than three finger-widths, from the middle of the eyebrow to the end of the navel, "of those who come and go," so it says.

With half of the maṇḍala one makes an eight-petaled lotus. Of its three parts, in the one part at the center is the sun seat. Upon it, Anantā and the king of Anantā, that is, Hevajra, treading upon them; and on the soles of the left and right feet of Hevajra one draws Jaya and Vijaya. Hevajra has eight faces, four feet, and is adorned with sixteen arms. Nairātmyā clings to his shoulder, and he is rich in twenty-four eyes, which will be explained.

Then on the petals of the lotus one draws the eight lords of the nāgas, with five hoods each, and the rest like the image of Ananta. On the left petal is Ananta. On the right is Vāsuki's son. In the west is Takṣaka. In the east is Karkoṭaka. In the southeast is Padma. In the northwest is Mahāpadma. In the southwest is Śaṅkhapāla. In the northeast is Kulika. At the center of these seed syllables is A Phu. On the petals to the right and left and so forth are phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ. With these eight syllables phuḥ one completes the eight nāgas. In the east and southeast they are black; in the south and southwest, red; in the west and northwest, yellow; in the north and northeast, white. This is by the distinction of the faces of mind, speech, wisdom, and body. All are drawn without the tail pressed down, lying stretched out.

Likewise the ground in the east is black, the south red, the west yellow, the north white, bounded by five lines. The eighth part of the maṇḍala is the gate; likewise the borders and the gate surrounds. Half of the gate is the platform and the ground of the lattice. Half of the platform has five lines, a jeweled cloth, bakulī flowers, and kramaśīrṣa. At three times the measure of the gate, the cornice descends; at the corners are variegated vajras. Outside is a garland of blazing vajras.

Having raised the maṇḍala in this way, one then performs the fire offering, in a round hearth, for the sake of pacifying. Then, having consecrated the maṇḍala, one offers the torma that will be explained, with the mantra beginning "Inda, Jama, Jala, Jakṣa." Afterward the master should recite the mantra with a wrathful inner mind. "Inner" means with a mind slightly joyful, so it says.

Then one places the gate guardians in the maṇḍala and sets out ten vases, and upon them places bowls filled with milk. Outside, in the ten directions, one hoists blue banners, and in a secluded place one recites this mantra: Oṃ ghaṭa ghaṭa, ghuṭu ghuṭa, maśa maśa, ghaṭa ghaṭa, ghoṭaya ghoṭaya, ananta kṣobhakarāya nāgādhipatāye, he he ru ru kaṃ, sapta pātālagatā nānāgana, ākarṣaya varṣaya, garjaya tarjaya, phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ, hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ, phaṭ phaṭ phaṭ svāhā. In this way the rain falls.

If the rain does not fall, one reverses it and recites this mantra: Hā svāṭa phaṭ phaṭ pha hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ, phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ, yarjata yarjaga yarṣabaya rṣaka, ā nāgānātāṃ galatāpāptasakaṃ ruru hehe yeta padhi gānāyarāka bhakṣonta na ayaṭa ghoyaṭa ghoṭa ghaṭa ghasa masa maṭu ghuṭu ghuṭa ghaṭa gha oṃ. When it has been recited in this way, if the nāgas still do not bring down rain, then their heads will split like the flower of the arjaka. Arjaka is a white-stemmed plant like black mallikā; just as its flower splits in the sunlight, so, it is certain, will they. This is the rite for bringing down rain.

Before this mantra one first recites it one hundred thousand times; by reciting it ten million times the mantra is accomplished. When one has performed one million fire offerings, then the practitioner, merely by offering the torma, causes the nāgas to bring down rain, and for that there is no maṇḍala rite. For the maṇḍala rite, one first performs the approach one hundred thousand times and ten thousand fire offerings, and then the nāgas bring down rain; in no other way, as is established in the root tantra.

Now the dispersing of clouds will be explained. Here, when the rain has become excessive, one places that Ananta, filled with charnel-ground ashes in a bowl. The mantra practitioner sits near a charnel-ground cloth and recites this mantra: Oṃ ārya śmaśāna priyāya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ phaṭ. With this he disperses them. Here too, as before, the approach, the fire offering, and the recitation are done. This is the rite for dispersing clouds.

Now the means of accomplishment of the Vajra Curved Knife will be explained. Here, in order to tame beings hard to tame, one accomplishes the mantra of the Vajra Curved Knife by reciting it ten million times, and also with one million fire offerings. Then one performs the practice with the skull. One pounds a skull together with the five nectars, adds kuṭhāra and chinna, and makes a pill about the size of a mustard seed.

Then one goes to the shrine of Heruka, and in front of Heruka, in the manner of a ten-fold abiding, one recites this mantra until, through the face of the deity's form, the pill is bestowed upon the practitioner: Oṃ vajra kartari he vajrāya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ. For the sake of accomplishment one recites a hundred thousand times, in a single sitting. Then it will be accomplished. Having accomplished it, one draws that figure on the neck of the pill. If one sees the figure on the neck of the pill, with the king and his retinue, and if that neck is cut, then the enemies will become headless. The Vajra Curved Knife is complete.

Now the means of accomplishment of the Vajra Axe will be explained. Here, wishing to split the gods of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), one accomplishes the bindu of the Vajra Axe. Here too the mantra is accomplished as before. Then, under the constellation Pushya, on the day of the full moon, one takes the seed of the brahma tree; the brahma tree is to be called palāśa.

Mix its seeds with kuṭhāra and cinna, in equal parts, and grind them with Akṣobhya. Having ground it, make an image of the enemy and let it dry. When the sun is seized by the eclipse demon, press it down with the foot and recite the mantra: Oṃ vajra kuṭhāra pāṭaya pāṭaya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. Having recited this ten million times, as before, then, when the sun is seized, accomplish it by day and by night, and having spread it out, draw a dot in the form of the enemy's image. Whatever god the practitioner honors with homage, that god is made to break apart. This is the rite for splitting a god.

Now I shall explain the means of accomplishing for fevers. Having accomplished the mantra as before, one who wishes to bring about a fever writes the enemy's name on a leaf of the arka plant, using poison, mustard seed, and the juice of citraka. Then dig a pit in the ground eight finger-widths deep. On top of it place a fire of husks, and recite ten thousand times: Oṃ hevajra jvara jvara śatruṃ bhrūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. Then the one to be accomplished is gradually afflicted. If the leaf is taken out and washed with cow's milk, he is freed. This is the rite for fevers.

Now I shall explain the rite for making someone vomit liquor. Having accomplished the mantra for making one vomit liquor as before, then, one who wishes to make someone vomit liquor visualizes in the navel of the one to be accomplished a wind maṇḍala transformed from the syllable yaṃ. Above it he visualizes a figure transformed from the syllable maṃ, with a belly full of liquor and with loosened hair, vomiting liquor; this is the one to be accomplished, vomiting liquor. He recites one hundred and eight times the mantra: Oṃ hevajra marya muṅgirāya maṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. This is the means of accomplishing for vomiting liquor.

Now I shall explain bringing under one's control. Having accomplished the Kurukullā mantra, as before, by the rite of reciting ten million times and so forth as explained earlier, then, one who wishes to bring a woman made utterly intoxicated under his control goes, on the eighth day, to beneath an aśoka tree, wearing red clothes and eating an intoxicating fruit. He makes a dot with kāmacikā juice and recites the mantra: Oṃ kurukulle hrīḥ amukāṃ me vaśī bhavatu svāhā. By reciting it ten thousand times she will come. This is the rite for bringing under control.

Now I shall explain seizing the moon and sun. First, having recited seven times ten million, the mantra practitioner accomplishes the mantra. Then, one who wishes to seize the moon and sun makes a moon or a sun from pounded rice, steams it, places it in a vessel four finger-widths across filled with vajra water, and recites the mantra: Oṃ vajrārka namācala mācala tiṣṭha tiṣṭha hevajrāya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. This is for holding the sun fixed. For holding the moon fixed, the mantra begins "Oṃ vajra candra," and so on. By reciting it ten thousand times, the moon or sun stays in place. When it is removed from the urine, it is freed. This is the rite for holding fixed the moon and sun.

Now I shall explain the entry into bringing down the pra, the vajra astrological calculation. As before, one accomplishes the mantra by reciting a hundred thousand times, ten times over, and by a hundred thousand fire offerings of red lotuses. The mantra is: Oṃ nagra nagra kumārike divya locani svāhā. With this mantra, at an unseasonable time, one praises the eyes of a young girl one hundred and eight times, honors them with the five offerings, such as flowers and incense, and then brings down the report. On the fourteenth or eighth day, in the morning, one sets down a vase, places on it a branch of a milk-bearing tree, and puts the fresh butter in a copper vessel, and the juice of resin in another vessel. One praises one hundred and eight times with the earlier mantra, smears the left thumb with resin and anoints it with sesame oil. Then, showing it to the young girl, the practitioner repeats the mantra and has her question the other, saying: "Tell me who has carried off my property. And what else do you see of my intentions?" Then she sees on the thumb and says: "A woman has taken the property, and the need concerns a woman." This is the rite for entering the bringing down of the pra for the sake of the vajra astrological calculation.

Now, to turn back livestock, I shall give the mantra of the essence of livestock. First one gives the syllable oṃ, in the middle the words of the mantra, and at the end "hevajrāya svāhā." One accomplishes it with a hundred thousand recitations, ten times over, and then applies it to the livestock. If one says "beṭuyā beṭuyā," the elephant flees. If one says "mammā mammā," the tiger flees. If one says "tellīyā tellīyā," the rhinoceros flees. If one says "ilimili phuḥ phuḥ," the snake flees. By the mudrā of Nor skyong dul ba, the dog flees. Oṃ śvā tiṣṭha tiṣṭha hevajrāya svāhā: this is the rite for turning back animals. In the same way, for the Ḍoms and others, the name of whichever one it is is placed in the middle, with oṃ first and at the end "hevajrāya svāhā." Reciting the mantra in this way and performing the fire offering rite will bring about accomplishments, but not by merely reading the book. This is the Blessed One's definitive statement in the root tantra.

Now I shall explain the deity yoga for the rites of paralyzing and the rest, beginning with "Vajrā and..." Here Vajrā is the aggregate of form, and by her one paralyzes. Gaurī is the aggregate of feeling, and by her one brings under control. Vetālī, the water yoginī, is the aggregate of perception, and by her one pacifies. Vajraḍākinī is the aggregate of formations, and by her one banishes. Nairātmyā is the aggregate of consciousness, and by her one certainly causes hatred. Bhūcarī is the vajra lady of tangibles, and by her one certainly summons. Khecarī is the vajra lady of the dharmadhātu, and by her one certainly kills. In the same way, Pukkasī, the yellow one, paralyzes. Śavarī, with the radiance of the moon jewel, pacifies. Caṇḍālī brings under control. Ḍombī banishes. The sovereign lady of the vajra expanse causes hatred. The element of wisdom accomplishes killing. In this way the yogin should know everything, outer and inner. With the colors yellow, white, red, black, green, and blue, one performs the paralyzing and the other rites: through the deity yoga, one accomplishes mantra recitation, fire offering, and the gathering of torma. This is the Blessed One's definitive statement in the root tantra.

This is the great summary that accomplishes the endless variety of rites, in the commentary on the six thousand verses of the Hevajra: the seventh section of the second chapter, the chapter on mantras. It is complete.

Wrathful and yet with attachment, in the form of supreme peace: in whatever form the lord of the Victors has meditated, in that very form he sees his own mind. All those forms are the mind of aspiration. The mind of entering is likewise that one mind. Mind that is not mind, the purity of the three liberations, the one who knows everything throughout the three times: having bowed my head to that Vajrasattva, I, Vajragarbha, shall write the commentary on the chapter on the deities, as realized from the root tantra, so that beings may attain the path.

The generation stage is one: it is focusing on beings and phenomena. The second, without focus, accomplishes the completion stage.

Here, in the Concise Tantra, the Blessed One said: "Now, from this, the chapter on the deities will be explained. First, one should meditate on Maitreya." By these and similar words, the initial words are to be applied to the root tantra, and so too the intermediate words and the concluding words.

There the Blessed One said:

By whom the distinctions of mantra and mudrā are mastered, the yogin's own self is accomplished. The sādhana and ritual procedure of the deity shall be explained by Vajragarbha.

At dawn the mantrin, having voided feces and urine and having done cleansing and the like, sits upon a soft seat. Having made a pill whose nature is the ten substances, he places it in his own mouth. With the five nectars and the elements, having made a maṇḍala before him, he offers incense, flowers and so on in order to Gaurī and the others. White Dbang to Yama, Caurī; in the direction of the lord of waters, Vetālī; to Kubera, the goddess Ḍākinī; below dwells Bhūcarī; above is taught Khecarī. To these one makes offerings in order. The goddess Īśāna's Pukkasī; likewise in fire, Śmaśānī; in the demons' quarter Caṇḍālī; in the wind, Ghasmarī; in order. In the center of the wheel, Hevajra is honored together with Nairātmyā.

Then one should recall loving-kindness, compassion and the rest toward all beings. Having honored these, one then places their seed syllables on one's own body. The seed syllable at the head of the name, together with the vowel a and the dot, is applied: Gaurī on the element of form; Caurī on the element of sound; Vetālī on the element of smell; Ghasmarī on the element of taste; Bhūcarī on the element of touch; Khecarī on the element of phenomena. On the eye, the goddess Pukkasī; on the ears Śmaśānī is intended; on the nose the goddess Caṇḍālī; on the tongue Ḍombī is intended. The body faculty is Hevajra; Nairātmyā dwells in the mind.

This is the first placing of the limbs, in the root tantra. The seed syllables gaṃ, caṃ, vaṃ, ghaṃ, bhaṃ, khaṃ, baṃ, śaṃ, caṃ, ḍaṃ, haṃ, naṃ should be placed on the elements as explained.

"Then one should contemplate guarding the place." It is thus: having meditated from Rephā for seven days, on that sun a hūṃ arises, a varied vajra; one should meditate also on the enclosure of that very vajra and the tent bound about it.

On this very point the root tantra says that first the protection wheel, which turns back the malicious, and the pinning down of obstructors and misleaders with the ritual dagger, are done by the mantrin in the body of the king of wrathful ones. Afterward the desired deity is to be accomplished. This tantra of Hevajra, whose nature is wisdom and means, is a yoga tantra; afterward, as the essence of Nairātmyā's sādhana, because it is practiced by the yoginīs, the yoginī tantra will be explained. Therefore the assembly of the yoga tantra, the lords of wrathful kings beginning with Yamāntaka, should be meditated upon in the protection wheel. The chief of the wheel is Vajra Hūṃkāra, who is the six-armed Hevajra.

Therefore, first one imagines a sun disk before oneself, and in its center the varied vajra, four-faced, wholly transformed from the syllable hūṃ. That varied vajra and the sun become mingled as one, and in all directions, above and below, there are the vajra fence, the vajra tent, and the vajra ground, which the demons cannot break. Outside the fence, and below and above, the mantrin should meditate on varied terrifying weapons.

Other matters should be applied from the root tantra. There the Blessed One said:

The seats of the wrathful kings I shall explain properly in order: faces, body colors, hands and weapons, and the feet in the stance with left leg extended. In the center of the vajra tent, from the syllable bhrūṃ fully transformed, a wheel of eight spokes arises. Below and above the tent two further spokes are made. With eleven hubs, in order, the sun disks; the eleven feet of the sun; these suns alone.

There, upon the sun discs, one places at each one the syllable HŪṂ. Arisen from the HŪṂ and abiding on the sun, one should meditate on the wrathful kings.

On the sun seat in the center, one envisions the leader, Vajrahūṃkāra. He is blue, with three faces and six arms, and he stands with the left leg extended in the posture of the striding stance. The central face is blue, with bared fangs, fierce and terrifying. The left and right faces are white and red, just as the three channels abide. In the right hands, in order, are the vajra, the curved knife with three points, and the bell; in the left are the skull cup and the khaṭvāṅga. He has hair of varied vajras, adorned with Akṣobhya and a half-moon. From his shoulders hangs a garland of heads, and on the head itself a skull. He possesses the five bone mudrās, and a tiger skin is wrapped about his waist. He has nine eyes, his tongue lolls, and he is adorned with serpent ornaments. Such is Vajrahūṃkāra, the leader; therefore one should meditate on him.

Arisen from a black HŪṂ, the one in the east is placed upon the sun: Yamāntaka, black and fierce, standing in the dancing posture with the left leg extended. His faces are black, white, and red, he has a great belly and three eyes, the five mudrās and a garland of skulls, and a tiger skin at the waist. He is marked with nāga lords and varied vajras. All of them have the family lord on the crown, and their colors and implements differ. Likewise, they have four feet each. In the right hands, in order, are the club, the wheel, and the vajra; at the heart is the threatening gesture, with the noose, the bell, and the axe in the left. On his crown is the Buddha Vairocana. The other one, too, is Vajra-crowned.

Arisen from a white HŪṂ, with faces white, black, and red, one places Prajñāntaka upon the sun in the south. He wears the jewel crown. In the right hands one should envisage the vajra, the wheel, and the club; in the left, at the heart, the threatening gesture and the noose, the bell, and the axe.

Completed from a red HŪṂ, with faces red, blue, and red, Padmāntaka is placed upon the sun in the west. He wears the crown of Amitābha. In the right hands he holds the pestle, the vajra, and the sword; at the heart, the threatening gesture and the noose, with the bell and the axe in the left.

Completed from a blue HŪṂ, wearing the crown of Amoghasiddhi, with faces blue, red, and white, with bared fangs and a terrifying countenance, and holding in the right hand the varied vajra, the wheel, and likewise the pestle; with the bell, the threatening gesture, the noose, and the axe in the left, one places Vighnāntaka upon the sun. In the north is Amṛtakuṇḍalin. Completed from a blue HŪṂ, with eyes blazing and flickering, he is placed upon the sun. His faces are blue, red, and white, and he wears the crown of the glorious Vairocana. He bears the sword, likewise the vajra, and the wheel in his right hands; at the heart, the threatening gesture and the noose, with the bell and the axe in the left.

Accomplished from a blue HŪṂ, with faces blue, red, and white, Ṭakkirāja is in the southwest, wearing the crown of Ratnasambhava. He holds the mudrā of Trailokyavijaya with his two hands; in the right, the vajra and the sword, and in the left, the threatening gesture and the iron hook.

Completed from a blue HŪṂ, with the head ornament of Amitābha, in the northwest, upon the sun disc, the mantrin places the blue Daṇḍa. The root face and the other faces are fierce, blue, red, and white. In the right hands, in order, are the club, the wheel, and likewise the sword; in the left, the threatening gesture, the noose, the lotus, and the axe.

Accomplished from a blue HŪṂ, wearing the crown of Amoghasiddhi, Mahābala is blue in color; the mantrin places him on the sun in the northeast, the lord of power. His faces are black, red, and white, his belly is great, and he is fearsome. In the right hands, in order, are the club, likewise the sword, the wheel, and the jewel; at the heart the threatening gesture and the noose, and in the left the three-pointed axe.

Accomplished from a green HŪṂ, with Akṣobhya as the skull, with faces green, red, and white, the chief figure has three eyes and a pendulous belly. With his left and right hands he holds the uṣṇīṣa on the crown. In the right hands are the vajra and the lotus; in the left, the threatening gesture and the noose. Above, he treads upon the sun in the sky. One should envision him in the realm of Brahmā.

Completed from a black HŪṂ, with Akṣobhya as the skull, below, upon the sun, is Sumbharāja. His faces are black, red, and white. In the right hands, in order, are the vajra, the wheel, and the jewel; at the heart, the threatening gesture and the noose, and in the left the lotus and the sword.

Having thus meditated on the assemblies of wrathful ones as fearsome, the mantrin then gathers them in and causes them to enter his own body. Yamāntaka is placed on the left shoulder, and Prajñāntaka on the right. On the left arm is Padmāntaka, and on the right Vighnāntaka is placed. On the left thigh is Acala, and on the right, the king of desire.

On the left foot, the one with the blue staff; on the right foot, the Great Powerful One. In the place of the head, the Great Uṣṇīṣa. Place the king Beautiful Harm-Doer at the anus. At the heart, make the vajra hūṃ, and place there the first letter of one's own name: yaṃ, praṃ, paṃ, baṃ, aṃ, ṭaṃ, naṃ, maṃ, uṃ, suṃ, hūṃ.

Now the rite of protection is this. The mantrin, having proceeded in due order, makes himself the vajra hūṃ, and then meditates on himself as wrathful. All obstructors and all evils he pins down with the daggers of the command of Beautiful Harm-Doer. Oṃ ghaghaghātaya ghātaya, sarva duṣṭān phaṭ phaṭ. Kīlaya kīlaya sarva pāpaṃ. Oṃ suṃbhani suṃbhani hūṃ hūṃ. Oṃ gṛhṇa gṛhṇa hūṃ hūṃ. Oṃ gṛhṇāpaya gṛhṇāpaya hūṃ hūṃ. Oṃ ānaya ho. Bhagavān vidyārāja hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ.

With this wrathful form, the wise one summons the obstructors and the vināyakas, and then, according to the rite, with this yoga he pins them all down with daggers. In the region below the navel, he should meditate one-pointedly on the great wrathful one, Vighnāntaka. Above, he has three eyes and six arms; below, he gazes upon the hosts of obstructors. One should recite his mantra, and with the vajra dagger strike the body of the obstructor so that it cannot move. Oṃ ghaghaghātaya ghātaya, sarva duṣṭān phaṭ phaṭ. Kīlaya kīlaya, sarva pāpaṃ phaṭ phaṭ. Hūṃ hūṃ vajra kīlaya. Vajradhara ājñāpayati: sarva vighnaṃ kāya vāk cittaṃ kīlaya kīlaya hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ.

Vajrapāṇi with the hammer, and the king Beautiful Harm-Doer, should be meditated upon. With this mantra, by the knowledge of the secret mantra, one should strike the vajra daggers. Oṃ vajra mudgara ākoṭaya ākoṭaya, tiṣṭha tiṣṭha. Having fixed the daggers in the ten directions, one should then drive away the malicious. Meditate that vajra flames blaze forth everywhere, and that the malicious ones flee, their bodies burning.

This brief tantra shows the protection wheel in a confused order. The practice from "Then, the yogin abiding above it," and so on, up to the pinning down with daggers, is to be meditated upon. This is the determination of the protection wheel. It is spoken of in four verses.

Then, in the ultimate sense, one should meditate that the three realms of existence lack intrinsic nature. In what has no real thing there is no meditation; meditation is not itself meditation. Thus a thing does not become a thing; meditation has no object. First, emptiness is awakening; second, one gathers the seed; third, the reflected image is completed; fourth, one places the letters. In this way, one should follow the yoga tantra of the protection wheel.

Then the mantrin, filling the expanse of the sky with the light rays from the hūṃ at his own heart, should imagine in the sky, as he is inclined, the venerable chief, embraced by his own wisdom consort and surrounded by the eight mothers, together with the gurus, buddhas and bodhisattvas. Then those light rays return, enter his own heart, and from the hūṃ he draws forth the eight goddesses in order to make offerings to the Blessed One. Here too, by reversing the usual order, there is one verse. From one's own body rays of light stream forth, pervading the maṇḍala of the sky. Gathering them back, one should draw them into the heart. The yogin becomes aversion itself, the vajra-born, of great compassion. Having gazed upon the venerable one in the sky, he offers to the eight goddesses, who wear all the ornaments.

Gaurī holds the sign of the deer. Caurī holds the vessel that conquers the māras. Vetālī holds the water-vessel. Ghasmarī holds medicine. Pukkasī holds the vajra in her hand. Likewise Śavarī holds the corpse. Caṇḍālī plays the small drum. Ḍombī dances with passion. The moon is to be called semen, the sun, dust; water is urine. Medicine is bone and flesh. Vajra Vairocana is declared. Corpse is said to be the six flavors. Gokus and so on are the powers, the small drum. Dance is the drink of beer. These are explained as the eight pledges. With these the yogin should make offerings to the chief Hevajra and his retinue.

Then, in front of him, the mantrin takes refuge in the Three Jewels. He should confess misdeeds, rejoice in merits, or offer his own body, and also become steadfast in the path. He generates the awakening mind. Then he requests the buddhas to depart. He directs his attention to emptiness. Again, he should examine in this way. Because of lacking intrinsic nature, it is empty; without cause, it has no sign. Always non-existent, there is no aspiration; volitional activity is abandoned. Because there is no arising or cessation, aspiration has no object. There are no buddhas, no sentient beings; there are no phenomena and no possessors of phenomena. Just as with illusion and dream, and with the city of gandharvas, so one sees buddhas and sentient beings through meditating on suchness. Just as with an echo, so the buddhas teach the Dharma.

Having examined thus, for the sake of taking up sentient beings throughout cyclic existence, the bodhisattva remains in existence. Though the afflictions, the māras, the equipoise, and the cognitive obscurations are certainly burned away by the great wrathful one, he preserves a portion of them. The great bodhisattvas, who love all sentient beings, having examined all buddhas and bodhisattvas completely, should direct their attention to emptiness and recite this secret mantra: Oṃ śūnyatā jñāna vajra svabhāva ātmako 'haṃ. Having recited this, he then recalls his aspiration. With the mind of aspiration, the mantrin should meditate, with divine pride, for the sake of the play of beings.

He should imagine the maṇḍala wheel that will be explained here, adorned with the eight charnel grounds. In its center he should meditate on a lotus with eight petals, a sun disc, and then, upon the sun, the corpse whose nature is the dharmadhātu. From the cause of the buddha's marks, the thirty-two vowels are on the moon, and the eighty consonants on the sun. In their center is the syllable hūṃ, which is called the being. It has the nature of innate joy, and from the sequence of mirror-like wisdom, wisdom of equality, and discriminating wisdom, these are accomplished as one in action. The form arises from the cause of complete perfection. This is explained as the dharmadhātu; the fifth wisdom is the supreme.

One who has fully awakened through the five aspects meditates on Hevajra with two arms. His color is a blend of blue and the pale gray of dawn, and his eyes are red like the bandhūka flower. He wears a garland of skulls and severed heads, and Akṣobhya is the crown set upon his head. He has the wheel, the earrings, the necklace, the bracelets, and the girdle. Through the purity of the five buddhas, the five mudrās are renowned. Akṣobhya dwells in the wheel. Amitābha is the very being of the earrings. The necklace is Ratnasambhava, the bracelets are Vairocana the Victor, and Amoghasiddhi dwells in the girdle. In this way the buddhas are fully established. The goddesses, Moharati (Mohavajrā) and the others, are the sense sources, the eye and the rest.

In his left hand are a vajra and a skull cup, and in the left too is the khaṭvāṅga. In the right is a black vajra, and his very nature is the uttering of the syllable hūṃ. In the charnel ground the Protector plays, surrounded by yoginīs. This knowledge, called the movement of the breath, is declared to be the charnel ground.

Such is the determination of the sādhana of the two-armed form. In the same way, the sādhana of the four-armed form and the sādhana of the six-armed form, up to the end of the chapter on the deities, are easy to understand. Therefore they are not written out here, since there is no difference. This is the eighth section of the sādhana of the deity taught in the commentary on the glorious Hevajra in six thousand lines. The chapter on the deities is completed.

The mirror-like wisdom, the wisdom of equality, and third, the wisdom of discernment; the other, the wisdom that accomplishes activity; and the purified dharmadhātu: these are the five wisdoms. Form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness itself, and earth, water, fire, and wind, with the fifth, space, are empty. The five wisdoms are purified, and the awakening through five aspects is thus fivefold. The one whose nature is the purity of the ten knowables, the holder of the purity of the ten knowables, is called the purified dharmadhātu. He is the great person, the great individual, himself possessing the form of the deity. Having paid homage to him, chief of beings, for the benefit of all sentient beings, I, Vajragarbha, will write the commentary on the chapter on empowerment, which follows the Root Tantra.

Here, the meaning of the chapter on empowerment in the Short Tantra should be understood through the meaning of the Root Tantra, and so one should enter into that very text. Here, when the form of the deity is complete, this mantra should be spoken: Oṃ suviśuddha-dharma-dhātu-svabhāva-ātmako 'ham. This is the completion of the deity's form.

Therefore in the Root Tantra the Blessed One declared:

Listen, Vajragarbha, as I explain
the stages of generation just as they are.
There are birth from an egg and birth from a womb,
the two stages that generate existence.
Birth from Brahmā's egg is called egg-birth;
the realms of the world arose in truth from it.
A woman gives birth from the womb as well;
the second is birth from the womb.

First is emptiness, awakening;
bliss comes at the end of death, in transference.
In the mother's womb are semen and blood;
the second is the gathering of the seeds.
Life-wind and consciousness, these five
complete the body.
It is to be spoken of as the completion of the image:
the full awakening through five aspects.
In the avadhūtī he swoons with bliss,
the outer sense sources are great enjoyment,
and the great bliss is what awakens them:
Pukkasī and Ḍombinī,
Caṇḍālī, and likewise Gaurī,
through the dream-state nature
of earth, water, fire, and wind.
Those who are childlike rouse it,
and emerging from the mother's womb,
they attain the waking state,
when the eyes and the rest are empowered.
From the awakening of the sixth consciousness
they grasp the five objects.

The lineage seals the child,
and the child seals the lineage as well.
The father seals the son,
and the son likewise the father.
The result seals the cause,
and the cause seals the result.
The relation of the effecting cause and the result
is not found when examined closely.
Therefore I will explain this examination of the effecting cause and the result; listen.

By the nature of seed and stalk,
the cause bears the mark of the result.
All this is imputed by the childlike.
The effecting cause becomes the result:
depending on the seed as cause,
the tree becomes the fruit-bearer, and likewise
depending on the tree-trunk as effecting cause,
the seed becomes the result.
Thus the cause becomes the result,
and the result too becomes the effecting cause.
The result and the effecting cause both come to be.
In this way these are four in number.
Without a seed there is no stalk,
without a stalk there is no seed.
Without an effecting cause, what result could there be?
The assertion would be meaningless.

The begetter of all buddhas
becomes in turn the buddhas' son.
That by which one becomes the aggregates, through bliss,
by that one becomes bliss again.
One becomes what is called Vajrasattva.
The aggregates are bliss from death.
Through those aggregates there is the begetter,
from the cause of death and transference.
Without bliss there are no aggregates;
without aggregates there is no bliss.
Without a cause there is no result;
without a result there is no cause.
Cause and result become two and two;
in this way these are four in number.
Who can fully examine
what is sealed there by the four?
Again, by the four times four,
through the dharmas of expansion and contraction,
since no fixed standing follows,
one remains within the existence of saṃsāra.

Through the nature of arising and ceasing, cause and effect remain in existence. Through the cessation of effect and cause, these neither arise nor perish. When cause and effect have ceased, how could one fail to become a buddha? The yogins who are freed from arising and ceasing see everything.

From sugarcane comes more cane; from cane again comes cane. In the same way, from merit comes merit, and from evil comes evil once more. As maggots arise from decay, so from evil there comes merit, and when merit is exhausted there comes evil, like worms in a dead body.

As the practitioner works with quicksilver, the stages of quicksilver are said to be five. In the same way the wise should know five stages in the awakening mind: the wafting of smoke, the sound "citi citi," the leaping of a frog, the trembling, and the absence of trembling. These are taught as the five stages of quicksilver. Likewise the awakening mind of the meditator passes through five stages: it changes, it changes again, then it flows, then it does not flow, and then it is free of stain.

As quicksilver is caught by a method resting on the cow's yellow pigment, so the awakening mind is held by a method resting on skillful means. Quicksilver that has not been made steady, a mind that has not been made stainless, serves the body and not liberation, serves wealth and not bliss. As copper, once transmuted by quicksilver, never becomes mere copper again, so the body transmuted by wisdom never returns to being an ordinary beast.

By the application of the best medicines, quicksilver consumes iron; made steady and transformed, it transmutes all irons into gold. So too, through the application of the reflection of emptiness, the constituents are consumed in bliss, and one who reaches stainless awakening blesses the minds of others.

Binding takes away disease; melting destroys poverty; transmutation bestows accomplishments; further transmutation gives deathlessness. Suitable causes take away old age, and ripening gives glory. This creator of persons brings accomplishments, and by being stainless it bestows deathlessness.

At death, ignorance enters, and from it the formations arise. This itself is taught as the first protector, three-faced and six-armed, Akṣobhya. Entering first, from it consciousness arises; from that come name and form; then the nature of the six elements, and the six sense sources. From these, form, vajra, and the rest arise without fail. The union of the eyes and the rest with forms and the rest is contact. From it feeling arises, which is Ratnasambhava himself. Craving is the nature of existence and enjoyment, and from that itself clinging arises. From that comes what is called grasping: the stretching out of hands and feet. From that comes becoming, which arises as the infant's breath coming forth.

From that there arises perception, and infants are always said to speak of what is permanent.
