Those bound by the habitual tendencies of conceptuality are first taught the rites of conceptual practice. When the nature of conceptuality is known, one enters into non-conceptuality. The meditation on the maṇḍala and the wheel, how the deities are generated, the summoning of wisdom, the empowerment, likewise the wheels, the channels, the seminal essence and the winds, all that was taught, the Protector of the World taught for the sake of drawing in the childish. The definitive meaning is practiced otherwise. Whoever looks at the manner of the maṇḍala, the deities, the summoning of wisdom, the empowerment, in terms of seminal essence and wind, that Hevajra does not know."

Now the guidance of the yoginī of the generation and completion stages of all tantras is shown as provisional and definitive meaning, gathered into six limbs. The six limbs taught at the stage of the completion stage are the definitive meaning; the six limbs taught in the generation stage should be understood as the provisional meaning. It is as follows: "First, meditate on black; second, meditate on red; third, meditate on yellow; fourth, green likewise; fifth, the color blue; sixth, the body of white. The yogī meditating the six limbs, and especially joy, and the end likewise." Here, at first, so that the childish may understand, the six limbs are Akṣobhya, Amitābha, Amoghasiddhi, Ratnasambhava, Vajrasattva, and Vairocana as the chief. These are also established as earth, water, fire, wind, space, and wisdom. Moreover, to abandon the conception of a single color, six colors are also taught, for the sake of the understanding of the childish.

Again, to draw in the Sāṃkhya followers, the six elements are shown through the nature of letters. The letters that sum these up are of six kinds: the five, a, i, ṛ, u, ḷ, and at the end of these, one of the letters of method or of wisdom, making six. This too is taught in the Recitation of Names: "The seminal drop, emptiness, the six letters." These six, transformed by being brought near to the consonants alone, which stand at thirty, become one hundred and eighty, and become the days of six months. Likewise, those that rely at the end on a letter of method or wisdom, transformed by bringing near the thirty, make one hundred and eighty; thus it is established as three hundred and sixty. Here, what is transformed together with the accent should be understood as wisdom, and what is transformed together with the seminal drop as method; this is established as the dharma of one year. The detail of this should be understood from the root tantra.

Likewise, for the childish who desire worldly accomplishments, the maṇḍala is meditated on in particular colors according to the kinds of activity, such as killing. Thus the wheel of secrecy is meditated as green, the emanation as yellow, the Dharma as black, the enjoyment as red, the glorious as white, and the crown protuberance as blue; these are words of the worldly vajra. By these statements the six limbs of generation are briefly shown.

"Free from joy, and the end likewise." This shows the six limbs of the completion stage. Thus: "The generation stage itself, and likewise the completion stage; abiding evenly in the two stages, the Dharma of the Vajra-holder is taught."

This is what was taught. And those six limbs of the completion stage are also set out in the eighteenth chapter of the glorious Guhyasamāja, where it says:

Withdrawal, meditative concentration,
Control of the vital breath, and likewise retention,
Recollection, and absorption:
These are called the yoga of six limbs.

This meaning should be understood in full from the Recitation of Names, the supreme primordial buddha. Furthermore, after guiding the immature, this very tantra teaches six limbs in the context of worldly conventional truth, as follows. The line "the aspect of generation is taught" means this: having taught the six limbs of the yoga of conceptual thought, which is the generation stage, the six limbs of union are now to be taught. The text says:

The element of space is the lotus;
Bhaga is said to be the wisdom.
Meditation is the union itself;
Its bliss is called the wheel.

Outwardly this is the element of space; inwardly, the lotus. Outwardly it is the triangular source of phenomena; inwardly, it is the experience of the special quality of the wisdom of bliss. Outwardly this is meditated upon through the sequence of vessel and contents, and inwardly it is the union of pairs. Outwardly it is the interior of the maṇḍala, and inwardly it is the wheel of the bliss of the four drops. That is expressed as follows:

Know it by yourself, in due order.

The outer wheel of the maṇḍala is the complete transformation of the four elements, earth, water, fire, and wind. Inwardly, the drops of the water, fire, and wind elements pass through a sequence that is divided by the sequence of the four instants, and so is analyzed by one's own awareness. The text goes on:

What is called "the gods" is the awakening mind;
How it arises is as the seminal fluid.

Within that palace, to realize in due order the bliss that arises from one's own aware seminal fluid is what the gods are. In this way the six limbs of union are taught.

Now, in order to teach the sequence of the four joys, the text says:

The coarse-born is of two kinds.
First, woman is wisdom itself;
Man should be called means.

For those who wish to attain buddhahood through the union of the two organs, man and woman are indicated by the terms means and wisdom, but this is not the definitive sense. The definitive sense is given in the line: "By the division into conventional and ultimate, afterwards these two are of two kinds." Therefore there are four joys. As to the symbol and what it symbolizes, the conventional bliss whose nature is emission is means, while the unchanging ultimate bliss is to be realized as wisdom. Otherwise, there is first the twofold man, as the text says:

The seminal fluid and its bliss
Are man; wisdom is likewise,
The seminal fluid and bliss itself.
Therefore the joys
Are the exact division into four.

The seminal fluid of men and women is means, and the bliss is wisdom. Likewise:

On the side of the completion stage also
Are these four innate ones.

This means that, setting aside the symbolizing aspect, on the side of the symbolized, the ultimate, there are four absorptions: the absorption gathered by wind and water, and the absorption gathered by fire and earth. This is why the Recitation of Names says: "Having the peak of the fourth concentration." As for those joys, the text says:

The first joy is the hero;
Supreme joy is the yoginī;
All the very blissful joys come from that;
Know it all from that bliss as means.

Because of the difference between the natural order and its reversal, in men the wind element is stirred first, and then fire, water, and earth in sequence. In women it is reversed, and earth is stirred first. The difference among those joys is taught thus:

Joy is slight bliss;
Supreme joy exceeds it;
Joy beyond joy is freedom from attachment;
The remainder is the innate joy itself.

This meaning is to be understood through these very words of the vajra that follow:

First, the wish to touch;
Second, the wish for bliss;
Third, the destruction of desire;
Therefore meditate on the fourth.

The vase, the secret, and the wisdom-knowledge initiations are by nature perishable and subject to destruction, so it is certain that one should meditate on the fourth concentration. Likewise the tantra of the supreme primordial buddha says:

The three are worldly and conventional;
The fourth is the ultimate itself.

It also says:

Supreme joy is called existence;
From joy beyond joy comes nirvāṇa;
The middle is mere joy;
Therefore the innate abandons it.

These lines should be taken in reverse order. "The middle is mere joy" refers to the first joy. Then comes supreme joy, and then the special joy. It is stated in reverse because the special joy is distinguished through the application of power; this should be learned from the guru's own mouth. The three joys described in this way are existence, that is, of perishable nature, and for this reason the earlier ones are set aside by the limb of wisdom through the division of the supramundane innate, and one should strive for the later states. Beyond even that is the innate of great wisdom, which is to be symbolized by all the earlier limbs of wisdom. This is what lies beyond the realm of expression, and yogins should take it up with effort. As the text says:

There is no desire and no freedom from desire;
It does not become an object of the middle.
Here there is neither means nor wisdom;
It is the true reality that appears.
Inexpressible by others, the innate.

The waxing and waning moon is desire and freedom from desire. The middle is the sixteenth part. In actuality it is not signified by sun, moon, and Rāhu, nor by man, woman, and neuter, nor by oṃ, āḥ, and HŪṂ, nor by parts, drops, or sound. Therefore those attached to the bliss of nirvāṇa will not find it apart from the guru, and so following the guru is taught:

It is not found anywhere;
The guru teaches the time and the means;
Know it from your own merit.

The symbol and the symbolized are to be known from the vase of the song of the precious guru, with the limb of the accumulation of one's own merit going first.

Having first found what is to be indicated, one should view it in this way:

The lowest, the middling and the highest,
and others, whoever they may be,
all of these, through meditating on that very sameness of taste,
should be seen as one and the same.

So it was taught; this is the brief teaching. By this vajra phrase, those who have directly found what is to be indicated abandon the subtle perception, the gross perception and the central perception of the three worlds, moving and unmoving, and are shown to indicate it as the unindicatable. As the root tantra also says:

The wise person who knows the vidyā,
having abandoned the syllables and the drops,
and indicated the unindicatable,
comes to hold the body of Hevajra.

So it was taught. In order to explain it extensively, the text says:

The lowest are the subtle meaning;
the highest is to be spoken of as the real thing;
this is what is abandoned by the two central channels.

This teaches the following. Either the things taught as unmoving, or the knowledge that views things as belonging to the desire realm, the form realm and the formless realm, or whatever things there are, highest, middling and lowest, that have the pattern of the three samādhis, one should abandon, and meditate on the sameness of taste of the innate. This is the meaning.

"Others" means the six faculties. In "whoever they may be, the stable and the moving," the meaning is that, of the stable and the moving, even the moving are not to be imagined as abiding in three kinds of things, because they are equal in the taste of the innate.

"Of all this there is no such thing" means that these three worlds of inner and outer phenomena are empty of self and other. This is the meaning.

Sameness is held to be likeness;
to meditate on that sameness of taste
is spoken of as "sameness," meaning likeness.
The taste is its wheel.

Here, by the stages of exposition just given, the wheel of existence of the three worlds, with its twelve limbs, arises in sequence. It is from this very wheel that the wheel of luminous bliss arises, so it is not to be sought elsewhere. This is the meaning.

For this very reason,
meditation is sameness, one taste.
By this meaning it is expressed.

So it was taught. The basis for making things the same and of one taste is the wheel of existence itself; apart from this I do not see any second thing at all. Therefore it is said:

From me all beings arise.
From me the three realms also arise.
By me all this is pervaded.
I see no other nature of beings.

This very wheel of luminous bliss remains, revolving, for the childish through deep sleep, waking, dreaming, and the bliss of sleep. It is the manifest emergence of the dharmadhātu. As the Mahāyāna sūtra also says:

For the holy it gives rise to bliss;
for the childish it shows terror.
If you see it, you will be freed;
if you do not see it, you will not be freed.

Therefore one should abide in the knowledge that existence itself is the supreme dwelling place of the buddhas and bodhisattvas. As the text says:

Having thus understood, the yogī
meditates in profound equipoise,
and even a person of little merit
will, without doubt, accomplish it.

This meaning is stated clearly in the root tantra, where it says:

Whoever abandons this wheel of existence
and seeks a supreme place,
that person, who commits the five heinous deeds,
is a wretch, and he is ruined.

The meaning is that the yogī who rests in equipoise without abiding in any such pattern will swiftly attain the accomplishment of Hevajra.

Eating, drinking, pouring offerings,
lying down, sleeping, and even thinking,
if one is intent on the great mudrā,
by that one will go to the supreme.

So it was taught. This meaning too is in the root tantra itself, where it says:

If one abides well in the great mudrā,
whether walking, strolling, or lying down,
and even if one commits the heinous deeds,
one will be freed; of this there is no doubt.
The yogī who is in this very state,
even if surrounded by a retinue of consorts,
I do not see as bound;
this very one is now Hevajra.

This is clearly taught in this very Condensed Tantra by this vajra phrase:

Where nothing is meditated on by the mind,
all beings come to be meditated on.
By the very meditation without meditation,
all phenomena should be fully known.

This phrase clearly explains the meaning of all the tantras, and of the two kinds of exhalation, it is the exhalation of the great vajra holder. The first of the six limbs of great wisdom, withdrawal, is what the glorious Primordial Buddha, the Guhyasamāja and others have designated by that term, and the line "accomplished by completely relinquishing all effort" is intended here. This very thing is the Perfection of Wisdom. This is Nairātmyā. This is Heruka. This is the buddhas of the three times. This is the one who liberates from the ocean of existence. This is the sūtra without letters. This is the definitive meaning.

Furthermore, this exhalation of the great vajra holder appears to childish people as a variety of things. They designate and abide in many conventional terms: the moving and the unmoving, self and other, life, nourishment, person, agent, the one who impels and the one impelled, father, mother, teacher, the ancestor of the world, earth, water, fire, wind, space, mountains, oceans, continents, forests, trees, creepers, vines and so on. Yet the protectors of the three times have never said that any of these exist other than as their own essence. The person who views these as supreme and holy is none other than the body of Hevajra. Therefore it says:

Whatever things there are, stable and moving,
grass, creepers, vines and the like,
from the nature of their own respective essence,
one should surely meditate on the holy suchness.
There is nothing supreme apart from these very things.

So it was taught. The meaning is that the essence of these things is neither something to be abandoned nor something not to be abandoned.

Regarding the lines, "Self-awareness is itself great bliss; from self-awareness awakening arises," the root tantra itself states this very point. As it says: "Vajragarbha, it is thus: leave the wheel of saṃsāra, whose nature is suffering, exactly as it is. For example, one makes no effort to paint the great expanse of space with many colors. In the same way, O Hevajra, a yogin who wishes to abide in the vajra should train in just this manner." That is the meaning.

The lines, "Self-awareness is the very reason for meditation; from the transformation of what is known by oneself, through suppression, action arises," show clearly both what is to be indicated and what indicates it. At the time of indication, through the words "the first joy, separated from it, has the supreme as its end," one makes the indicator manifest by means of intelligence, and this is the meaning of "one should meditate on what is known by oneself." Therefore the indicator itself, once suppressed and brought under control, becomes clearly manifest. For this reason, as it says: "Self seizes and self acts; self is the king, self is the chief." These words speak of distinctions, and they are the words of the vajra at the time of indication. Such an indicator is to be sought out with great effort at the very beginning; later it is abandoned through the distinction of meditative concentration. For the Chanting of Names says: "Having abandoned the desire for existence and the rest, he is the great joy of the three worlds; white like pure white clouds, radiant like the light of the autumn moon, beautiful like the disk of the rising sun."

Therefore what indicates is the desire for existence, which arises through the power of all the afflictions. What is contrary to it is that which transcends all examples; this is the attainment of the manifest weapon, which is a superior distinction. Hence this same Condensed Tantra says: "Desire, aversion, envy, and likewise delusion and pride, all these do not match even a sixteenth part of the joy that abides there." Likewise, "The wisdom born from the source of phenomena" refers to the bliss that relies on the source of birth. "Equal to space, together with the means" means by the means of undefiled bliss. Elsewhere it is also said: "From the nature of means and wisdom, the three worlds are born from that." Just from the bliss of elaboration the three worlds arise, and the unchanging wisdom too arises from it. For this reason, depending on the context, the terms "father and mother," or "means and wisdom," are applied. In the context of this great secret, the designation is made by distinguishing support from supported. "The form of semen is the Blessed One; his bliss is called the mother." Semen is the father, and bliss is to be understood as the mother. Why is wisdom described in terms of bliss? Because this very text says: "From non-existence, that which is without entity is wisdom; the entity is called the means." And likewise, bliss is cut by the net of all conceptions. It is free from one and many, as in "greater joy than a single instant." This is to be understood, and so it comes to be known through the division of the four instants and the parts of the four joys.

How is it known? "This wisdom of one's own awareness is beyond speech and is a domain of experience. Because this is the sequence of blessing, such is omniscient wisdom. Earth, water, wind, fire, and space as well: through the feeling of awareness that is their nature, not even an instant of any of them can harm. The heavens, the human realm, and the lower realms become a single form in an instant." So, by distinguishing outer and inner, whoever abides in the meditative concentration of bliss, which transcends all examples, is not harmed by those who dwell beneath, upon, and above the earth in the three worlds. For there is nothing that, in that instant, does not become unchanging blissful wisdom. Moreover, these three realms do harm because one is bound by the iron chain of grasping at self and at what is mine. But "by the conceptions of self and other, this great lord is not made subject to harm." That is the meaning.

Here, most childish people, those who belong to the world, hope to be liberated by the four Vedas, by many tenets, and by the eight worldly accomplishments and the like. This the great ancestor has refuted. As it says: "All Vedic tenets, and likewise the increasing of rites and so on, are not accomplishments that purify existence; one will again be born in existence." This is the definitive meaning. "In this world and the next, without that there is no accomplishment." Thus, the childish, inferior in accomplishment because they lack the inexpressible, wander and wander about, and as a result their suffering never ceases. Therefore, for the sake of liberation, one should not abide in desire and attachment to the eight great worldly lords and the like.

"One who does not know this, O Hevajra" means one who does not know Hevajra as just taught. "His toil becomes meaningless" means that, lacking Hevajra, who has no sign of elaboration, even if one practices with body, speech, and mind beyond the count of eons, one is without result.

"Rushing down like the current of a river" refers to those who abide on the path, such as the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; they are like a river. Otherwise, the childish look down at the current of the Gaṅgā flowing day and night, and conceptions of signs arise. "Bound like the tip of a lamp's flame" means that a yogin abiding in the two paths also comes to abide in the path of fixation, and so cuts off his own path. In the same way, the river that looks downward should be turned into the path of non-duality, like the tip of a lamp's flame, just as waters of differing color, taste, and shape, such as those of the Gaṅgā and the Sitā, become indistinguishable once they have entered the outer ocean. "Always set that very thing, through yoga, in day and night" means: place yourself in that suchness, the single taste of all things, in every mode of conduct. That is the meaning.

Here, what is the use of speaking at length? Whatever becomes an object of the intellect, let one meditate on all of it as having a single flavor. Then accomplishments pervading like space will arise. Thus it is said.

This is the eighth chapter, the extensive explanation of the wheel of yoginīs, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Having paid homage to the one who holds the vajra, who arises from the nature of non-entity, I, Vajragarbha, will write the extensive explanation of the chapter on purification.

Now, a poison or other substance that has not been purified only kills. Therefore, intelligent persons should purify it. So, by the method of the three great secrets, one should purify the three: body, speech, and mind.

First, the text says, "Then the chapter on supreme purification will be explained." This means that it follows upon the explanation of the wheel of yoginīs.

"Certainly all things": this means all things belonging to the three realms. "Purity is declared to be suchness": the medicine that removes the poison of things is suchness itself, which does not change.

Moreover, so that children, the immature, may understand it, the text says: "Afterward, one by one, by way of distinctions, they are to be declared as deities." As to the generation stage, the mantra beginning "Oṃ śūnyatā" and so on is certainly the purification of all things. In the same way, what is imagined as the palace, the deities, and the like is of provisional meaning. This is just what the text says: "Afterward, one by one, by way of distinctions, they are to be declared as deities. The five aggregates and the six faculties, the six sense sources and the five great elements, are purified in their own nature."

First, the nonmoving elements are purified by being imagined as the palace, and the moving elements by being imagined as the deities. Of these, "the five aggregates" means that the five aggregates, from form to consciousness, are purified as the five beginning with Vairocana. In the same way, the faculties, the sense sources, the great elements, and so on are purified as the bodhisattvas and the five consorts.

"The obscurations of affliction and of the knowable are to be purified. Self-awareness, one's own very nature, is itself purity. Purity is not liberation by anything else." The purpose of purifying is to purify the afflictive and cognitive obscurations. How, then, are they purified? The text says "self-awareness, one's own very nature." The knowing that form and the rest are by their own nature unborn is called purity. That purification is not to be done by another way, since not realizing this is the definitive meaning.

Therefore, for that reason: "Because objects are purified as things, the supreme bliss is self-awareness. Objects such as form, and also whatever else is seen by the yogī, are all these things purified. In this way, beings are buddhas by nature." In order that children may understand, form and the rest are by their own nature the buddhas, the chief ones, and so on, as the words "form is Vairocana" and the like declare. Therefore the objects that appear and serve a function, such as the wish-fulfilling tree, the excellent vase, the wish-granting jewel, and the sword, are purified, and this is taught to children as a provisional meaning.

"Blessed One, what is impure?" Vajragarbha made this request to the Blessed One. The meaning is: earlier the Blessed One said that objects such as form, and faculties such as the eye, are buddhas from the very beginning; so I have not seen any second thing apart from that.

"The Blessed One said": this is the reply to Vajragarbha. "Form and the rest": this means that these very things, form and the rest, are impure for children, because they kill in the manner described.

"Why is that?" Vajragarbha asked again. "Because of the things grasped and the grasper": ordinary people grasp at a self and what belongs to a self. Thus they grasp a person as having a nature of his own, and likewise they grasp at change, and at sound, praise, expressive words, and so on. This is what is meant.

"Blessed One, what are the grasped and the grasper?" This asks: by what, at what, and how does one grasp? The Blessed One said in reply: "With the eye one grasps form; with the ear one listens to sound; with the nose one smells scent; with the tongue one tastes flavor; with all things one touches." Children enter into objects in the manner of object and subject, self and what belongs to self, pleasure and pain, and so they are impure. The skillful yogī enters into those same things by means of skillful method, and through this is purified. All pleasure and pain are empty and secluded by their own nature from the beginning. One who knows it so is purified, and this is the definitive meaning.

Therefore: "Those who obtain pleasure and so on through the mind, having rendered them free of poison through purity, should partake of these things to be enjoyed." Here the skillful yogī and those of childlike nature rely on the two truths in turn. The skillful yogī too does not abandon conventional truth: with the twelve illusion examples and the wheel of samaya, each shows the wheel of the maṇḍala according to how each is to be trained.

Likewise, a beginner is to reverse the grasping at self and what belongs to self, that is, the clinging to clan, lineage, cause, and caste. The so-called vajra family rests on the seven conventional empowerments. In order to reverse gradually the conceptions of pure and impure, the four families may mingle among themselves; foods such as ku and the rest are eaten in common; one is to approach those of one's own family and those not of one's family; and one is to dwell together with mothers, sisters, and the like. Through this one turns away from the very coarse ordinary conceptions. Therefore the vajra family gains its name through the seven empowerments themselves.

Therefore we should conceive of the aggregates, the elements, the sense sources, the faculties, the great elements, and whatever is derived from the great elements, and likewise actions and the activities of the faculties of action, as nothing other than deities; we should not conceive of them in any other way. Thus beginners among yogins should proceed as it is said:

The form aggregate is Vajrā; and feeling is spoken of as Gaurī. Perception is Vārī, the yoginī of water; formations are Vajra Ḍākinī. In the manner of the aggregate of consciousness, the yoginī Nairātmyā abides. When these are constantly purified, the yogin accomplishes reality itself.

This is just as a crystal jewel takes on whatever color is placed near it. The great Vajradhara also said to the bodhisattva called Vajradhātu, "Son of good family, the mind becomes just as it has been trained." For this reason the five aggregates are to be conceived as the five goddesses. How this is taught in the inner mode is explained at length in the Yoginī Cakra. That is the inner addition, the purification of the five aggregates.

Because the objects too are to be conceived as deities, the outer addition is then explained. In the northeast, Pukkasī; in the fire quarter likewise Śavarī; in the southwest place Caṇḍālī; in the wind quarter establish Yungmo. By this the goddesses of the four elements are shown.

Likewise, the objects, form and so on, are to be conceived as the deities of the four elements, as follows: in the northeast Gaurī, in the south Caurī, in the direction of water Vetālī, and in the northwest Ghasmarī. This is what is taught.

Further: "Below, she is called Bhūcarī; above, she is declared to be Khecarī." This was explained at length above in terms of outer and inner. The phrase "the aspect of the generation stage" means that it is taught in this order of presentation for beginners.

As for the phrase "by the nature of existence and peace," existence is Khecarī and peace is Bhūcarī. As another tantra says, existence is the forehead, and nirvāṇa is what drips from the throat of the jewel. "These two goddesses are established" should be understood as the two goddesses above and below, in the nature of existence and peace.

What is taught as purity within the generation stage, for the childish to understand, is this: form is taught to be conceived as Gaurī; sound is known as Caurī; the portion of smell is Vetālī; and taste is known as Ghasmarī. Having cultivated an inclination toward these four objects, as conceived by the childish, one accomplishes, on the conventional level, such acts as killing.

The definitive meaning of these four actions is taught in the root tantra. Pacifying is when Vajragarbha calms the torment of the afflictions by the water of awakening mind that arises from the body of embodied beings. Enriching is because body, speech and mind increase through an unprecedented, overflowing bliss. Controlling is because body, speech and mind are turned back from suffering and brought under the power of bliss. The fierce rite is because, by that power, it kills the movement of the twenty-one thousand six hundred life-winds, and the movement of the drops of awakening mind that look downward. Likewise it is said: pacifying is meditation on water, contemplated at the forehead; controlling is meditation on fire, contemplated at the throat; the secret, the meditation on wind, at the heart; enriching, the meditation on earth, abides at the navel.

As for "by touch she is called Bhūcarī; in the dharmadhātu she is Khecarī," this is in terms of outer and inner: consciousness, called the space element, the life-wind moving from above, is taught as the reality of the yoga tantras, the reality of the Guhyasamāja and the others, and is taught as the father's mind. Likewise Bhūcarī is called the element of unchanging supreme wisdom. It is the bliss of the drops of semen dripping downward in embodied beings, the reality of the yoginī tantras, taught as the reality of the Hevajra and the others, and taught as the mother's mind.

This is also said in the tantra called the Primordial Buddha: "The two channels of mind, above and below: one carries the falling seed and is dark; the mind of method is dark; the falling seed belongs to wisdom." Understand this meaning through this vajra word.

Likewise, "When these are constantly purified, the yogin accomplishes reality itself" means that, by attending in due order according to the provisional and the definitive meaning, one will attain in sequence the reality of the worldly and the transworldly.

Further: "The purity of the hands is emptiness; the purity of the māras is the feet; the faces are the eight liberations; the purity of the eyes is the three vajras." In due order this refers to the sixteen emptinesses, which have the character of the waning moon; the cessation of the four states, such as waking, belonging to worldly beings; the wheel of Dharma, characterized by the eight watches; and the syllables Oṃ Āḥ Hūṃ, characterized by body, speech and mind.

Further: "Earth is called Pukkasī; the water element is called Vyādhī, the huntress; fire is to be known as Caṇḍālī; wind is widely known as Yungmo." This is to be understood outwardly and inwardly in terms of the four kinds of action. The definitive meaning is as the root tantra itself says: "Life is to be cut by the vajra family; Amoghasiddhi speaks falsehood; one steals with the jewel family; one engages in unchastity with Amitābha." Understanding this as it is, it is intended as the cause of the two siddhis.

Likewise, by the distinction of principal figures and ordinary ones: "Aversion, it is said, is Vajrā. Desire is the yoginī of water. Envy is the vajra ḍākinī. Miserliness is the secret Gaurī herself. Likewise, delusion is Mohavajrā." This means that, in due order, these are to be realized in Akṣobhya, Ratnasambhava, Amoghasiddhi, Amitābha, Vairocana and the others, by means of the two classes of yogī. Yet it is for the sake of realization by yogīs of limited capacity, the childlike, that this was taught in this context. How is that evident? Because it was said: "In the aspect of the generation stage, which purifies aversion and the other afflictions, the aggregates are to be purified by this."

Even skilled yogīs, following the order thus expounded and relying on the manner of the two truths and the two stages, should teach the Dharma in whatever way the minds of the childlike will be liberated. This is the certainty of the Tathāgatas. Therefore, even in this present age, when strife is rampant, for those who are fit to be tamed through desire, the teaching of desire has been given. "Whatever binds the world, by that very thing one is freed from bondage. The world that does not know this very thing is not freed; and one who abandons it altogether will not find accomplishment." This was taught because the heroes who possess great compassion saw that very method as supreme. Therefore, by these words and letters the childlike are first led onward, but it is not to be taken up by others.

As it is said in this very tantra: "Just as when water has entered the ear, it is drawn out by other water; or again, when one is burned by fire, one is warmed by fire again; so too, when burned by the fire of desire, one should be warmed by desire itself." Therefore it was taught: "No scent, no sound, no form at all; no taste, and no purity of mind; no touch, and no phenomena; all is utterly pure. Know that beings go to the realm of naturally pure beings." That is, first the nature of the afflictions is shown at the outset, and afterward those very afflictions are gradually abandoned by skillful means. This is the instruction of the Tathāgatas. And in the root tantra too it is said: "By whatever sound and whatever guise beings are to be tamed, by that very sound and that very guise the vajra-holders teach the Dharma." And again: "Brahmā, Maheśvara, Viṣṇu, the god of desire, the fierce one, Skanda and the others: these too, in their forms, are for the moment praised; they are the means by which the world draws breath." And again: "O sons of good family, the afflictions such as the union of two are not by their own essence the nature of the afflictions; they are taught as afflictions because of the differing dispositions of persons." All these points should be understood in this way.

This is the ninth chapter, the extensive explanation of the chapter on purification, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Hevajra.

Homage to the Vajra Protector, whose essential body, enjoyment body and complete form, and whose emanation body, endowed with the marks, bestow the four accomplishments.

For the benefit of practitioners, the stainless extensive explanation by which disciples are consecrated has been well taught by Vajragarbha. Then, how the order of the maṇḍala is rightly taught: this is introduced by the words "Then..." Here, the vajra master, so that he may bring a disciple into the maṇḍala, must first dispel the obstacles arising from the conceptual thought of both himself and the disciple. This is to be done in this way: the rite is to be begun on a favorable day of the week, an auspicious lunar day, in conjunction with an auspicious constellation, and at an auspicious time. This is the certainty of the Tathāgatas: it is to be begun only after the distinctions of the worldly and transcendent empowerments have been thoroughly gathered in the mind, and not otherwise. That is the meaning.

"I shall fully explain the rite by which the disciple is consecrated": this is the summary statement. In order to introduce the vajra master beforehand, the text says: "First, the actual deity is performed. From HŪṂ the vajra is made with effort. The yogī purifies the ground, and afterward draws the maṇḍala." This means that the vajra master himself first generates himself as the wrathful Hevajra, victor over the three realms, one-faced and two-armed, from the seed syllable HŪṂ. He makes the protection wheel and the other preliminaries, thoroughly purifies the ground, and enters the maṇḍala of meditative absorption himself. That is the definitive meaning.

To show the particular qualities of the site, the text says: "In pleasure groves, in secluded places, in the houses of bodhisattvas, in empty houses, or in a temple, the supreme maṇḍala is to be drawn." Such places are auspicious, delightful, and bring accomplishment swiftly, so they should be sought with effort. At that site one builds a raised platform of earth to whatever size is desired, marks out the lines flawlessly with the measuring cord described in the tantra, and then lays down the colored powders.

"One draws with the supreme colored powders, or else with the intermediate ones: powder of the five jewels, or rice flour and the like." Colored powders are of two kinds. Powder of the five precious substances, such as coral, is taught as supreme. If these cannot be obtained, one may also draw with rice flour and the like dyed with pigments. That is the meaning. As for "the maṇḍala should exceed three cubits by three finger-breadths," the measure is shown either through the three of body, speech and mind, or through the manner of dust, darkness and vitality.

"There, the excellent consorts born from the five families are to be placed." That is, having drawn the maṇḍala according to the rite, the mantra-holder, who himself possesses meditation like the moon's reflection in water, blesses the maṇḍala of colored powders and enters it well himself. Afterward, he has the disciples first take the restraint of the fasting vow, the outer pledge vows, and the generation of the supreme awakening mind. After that he bestows the worldly empowerments according to their distinctions. This is so that the childlike may be led. The empowerment called the vajra master's empowerment is to be completed in full.

Then, in order to show the path of desire to the childlike, a young girl bearing the signs of the five families, devoted above all to the secret vehicle, is first well trained, as will be explained below, and then she is brought into the maṇḍala. As for how: those who possess the age of the sun, those who possess the age of the king, and those who possess the age of twenty are distinguished according to the divisions of the wheels of body, speech and mind, and are brought into the maṇḍala. While one who possesses the age of the sun is available, one of the age of the king or the like is not brought in. But if none who possesses the age of the sun can be found, one possessed of the age of the king and so forth should be brought in. For it is said: "Or else, whoever is found, just so, one who has reached sixteen." For the mudrā of the age of the sun, which bestows supreme bliss, is taught as the best; the age of the king is middling; and the age of twenty is declared the lowest.

"As long as semen is present, so long should the mudrā be relied upon." This should be understood in two ways. The bliss that becomes a cause of happiness for women arises from the age of the sun, and not earlier than that. Likewise, once twenty has passed, that very bliss is destroyed. Moreover, the bliss that comes to be for the vidyā-mantra holders and the vajra disciples should be relied upon for as long as it is experienced. Therefore it is said so.

"The mudrā's face is also bound, and the face of the means is likewise." By this it is meant that the girl, the man and the one receiving empowerment cover one another's faces; this is the armor.

"What arises from that teaching should be dropped into the disciple's mouth." The master, the holder of vidyā-mantras, having relied on the young woman as is proper, mixes it equally with vermilion, with a mind abiding in the supreme delight of the secret, and applies it fully as enjoyment through the disciple's mouth. That this is to be shown as the nature of speech is itself what is proclaimed as secret.

"The very domain of the disciple of equal taste: that too should be done there. It will become self-aware wisdom." Having thus realized what is called the secret, and having shown the reality of worldly desire just as it is, one has the subtle junction of the kings and lords of the mind placed in the site that is examined as the place where it is indicated. This is the command of Vajradhara. After that, the subtle substance is released, a great offering of the gathering is made, and all the treatise on desire that comes below is carried out. Then, from the great guru, the holder of vidyā-mantras, one should seek with life and effort the stages of accomplishing the milk that lies beyond that. Then, regarding that in which the indicator is made clear, it is said: "It will become self-aware wisdom." That which shows and clarifies in this way the reality of the wisdom of discernment is the work of the vidyā-mantra holder; remaining firm and avoiding the realm of Māra is the work of the disciple. Thus, one abandons the self-aware wisdom that dwells far away like an old man, and enters the great wisdom.

As is said in the root tantra: "Abandoning self-aware wisdom, what cannot be indicated is the indicated; abandoning sound and drop, beyond letters is the characteristic." And likewise in the Recitation of Names: "The supreme person, who knows himself and others, benefits all; beyond all examples, the supreme lord of knowing and the known." Therefore it is certain that one abandons the self-aware wisdom of worldly kind and meditates on the great bliss that does not change. Hence: "Abandoning the right knowing of self and other, emptiness equal to space and free of dust, the supreme nature of existence and non-existence, thoroughly blending means and wisdom, blending attachment and freedom from attachment." This shows that worldly desire is perishable, changing, aware of only itself, one-sided, divided in nature into existence and non-existence, called means and wisdom only by an improper designation and not truly blended, and its aspects of attachment and freedom from attachment are widely divided. Therefore, abandoning that, one must meditate on the unchanging bliss called the fourth.

Therefore, this very great innate wisdom draws embodied beings out of saṃsāra and leads them to the state of holding awareness; it is the great exhalation of breath. As it says: "That alone is the life of living beings; that alone is the supreme unchanging; that alone is the self of beings; that alone is the all-pervader." The great unchanging bliss is the supreme secret of the body, speech and mind of all buddhas, which refutes and subdues all the views of ordinary worldly people regarding a self. Likewise, for those in saṃsāra it is realized through the semen abiding in the body; without semen it cannot be realized. To realize in reliance on semen is the way of the Dharma of desire. Hence it is said: "It abides entirely in the body."

Likewise, those who realize existence and non-existence arise from that very semen of awakening mind. For from semen directed downward arises the basis of grasping at self and what belongs to self; but from that same semen directed upward and increased, one realizes the condensed and expanded forms of all phenomena. Therefore: "Existence and non-existence arise from that; others and whatever they are; the way of all consciousness; the primordial person, the Lord, the self, the living being, sentient being, time, the person, the nature of all things: this abides truly in the form of illusion." Thus, from the downward flow of the semen of awakening mind, it is known that tenets, all views, poetry, histories, and the doctrines of fortune-tellers, of Īśvara, of agents and so forth, many such arise. Therefore the support of the body should be guarded with effort, and those who abide in this way should know all as like illusion. This is the intention.

In order to realize that very illusion-like nature, it is said: "The first is simply joy; the second, counted as such, is supreme joy; the third is freedom from joy; the fourth is called innate." Here, the seminal essence that dwells in the body, when it is relied upon inwardly in the manner of the four joys, brings about the attainment of the illusory body. This is taught as the very way of the dharma of desire, and it is the word of the Tathāgata.

Moreover, in the great tantra called the Vajra Garland it is said: "The unchanging nature of one's own body is to be known through the one supreme one. Having been indicated through an example, it is to be known by the kindness of the guru." Likewise in the Net of Illusion it is said at length: "Having sixteen half-parts, with drops; beyond the reckoning of limbs; having the summit of the fourth meditative concentration; knowing all the limbs of meditative concentration," and so on.

"Vajragarbha and all the others, having heard the wise one declare this, became supremely astonished, fainted, and collapsed upon the ground." This means that when the Blessed One Hevajra taught this dharma to Vajragarbha and the others in the manner of desire, they were utterly amazed and fell to the ground. For the Blessed One's words taught that the very thing which in some contexts is taught as binding is what liberates, and he did not tell them to seek buddhahood anywhere other than in their own bodies. When told, "Buddhahood is in this very body," they said, "Yet we have still not realized it." In this way the Blessed One displayed his miraculous power.

Then they asked the Blessed One Hevajra: "By what stages should we train in that very desire, and how should we strive?" The Blessed One answered: "At first, joy is the form of going; likewise supreme joy is itself going; freedom from joy, and joy too, is itself going; the third has no innate. The Blessed One declared to Hevajra that all buddhas have a single body. So that Vajragarbha might realize it, this is the dispelling without doubt: there is no desire and no freedom from attachment; it does not come to be observed in the middle. Because the three are abandoned, the innate is called awakening."

Regarding that dharma in the manner of desire: the bliss that is the taste of the four instants, which for the childish are distinguished only in four aspects, is to be known from the vajra master, the holder of knowledge-mantras. And it is thus. In accordance with the word of the holder of knowledge-mantras, the three joys are wholly discarded when the three existences are generated, and the fourth is certainly to be meditated upon. In this way, the bliss characterized by the three joys, namely the vase, the secret, and the wisdom-gnosis empowerments, is worldly bliss, since it is only conventional. One discards it, and the bliss that is brought inward, free from desire and from attachment, beyond even the middle, bestows the conduct of bliss itself and liberation.

"Or that very one is all the self; or it is the abandonment of everything." This means that all four worldly joys are abandoned, and all four supramundane joys are to be meditated upon without exception. What well indicates this beyond the four worldly ones is the teaching that one takes on being completely filled by the arising of all four joys. And so it is said: "Freedom from joy is indicated first; the three joys are wholly abandoned."

Thus, when one has abandoned those worldly bliss-like experiences, and at that time abides in the great innate, one has passed beyond the stage of the beginner. It is said: "At first it is like a cloud; when accomplished it becomes illusion-like." At first, the absorption of bliss alternates between being clear and unclear. Afterward, because the absorption of bliss is clear, it becomes predominant, and the unclear is overpowered. Likewise: "Through the distinction of sleeping and lying down, it is always like a dream." This means that after these, at all times, in every state of sleep and waking, all phenomena abide as illusion-like, and no occasion at all is given to the signs of conceptual thought. This is the sign that stability has been attained.

Likewise in the root great tantra it is said: "Having passed beyond the first stage, in what absorption does the yogin abide? The signs of that absorption should be known as of three kinds." Those three signs are the indications of drawing close to awakening. Therefore it is said: "Accomplishment, whose characteristic is inseparability, is achieved by the yogin through the mudrā."

Now, to present what was mentioned earlier, namely the characteristics of the maṇḍala, its shape and so on, the characteristics of the measuring thread, of the colored powders, and the vases and substances, it is said: "The Teacher spoke of the maṇḍala thus: square, blazing brilliantly, with four doors of great splendor, adorned with half-garlands upon garlands, with various wreaths and banners, beautified by eight pillars, and equipped with vajra threads of equal measure." The literal meaning of this is easy to understand.

Further: "Well adorned with various flowers, with incense, lamps, and likewise fragrance, equipped with eight vases and so on, along with branches and so forth; the neck should be covered with cloth, and the five jewels are placed within." These and the following lines present the implements of the maṇḍala and are easy to understand.

"Give Vijaya in the east." This means that one consecrates the vase of all rites, and the water consecrated with the mantra of the wrathful one who is the enemy of obstructors is poured into it, and it is placed in the east of the maṇḍala chamber.

"By the form of one's chosen deity" means by the yoga of one face and two arms.

"Newly and well spun, and wonderfully fine in measure, the thread is to be laid down by one with wisdom." This means that a wise mantrin should fold clean white cotton cloth into five layers, then dye each separately with colors matching the five tathāgatas. Having consecrated it according to the rite as the five families of victors, he should lay the line without error in the directions.

In the same way, the deities of the maṇḍala are to be propitiated. This is what is meant by the lines: "One hundred thousand recitations for the lord of the wheel, and likewise ten thousand for each of those of the maṇḍala." For the sake of propitiating the maṇḍala, the vidyā-holding master first recites the root mantra of the principal deity one hundred thousand times, and then recites ten thousand times for each of the deities of the maṇḍala, thereby pleasing them. He also offers tormas to the guardians of the directions at the three times of day. After that, he carefully examines the characteristics of the site for the pit, and purifies the ground in the manner of the ritual of the great maṇḍala. As it is said: "With the very mantra spoken above, the wise one should purify the ground; with the mantra beginning with A, as before, he should present the torma there." This is to be understood here just as it was explained earlier, when the maṇḍala of the principal deity was taught. In the same way, the protection wheel, the purification into the divine pride of one's own deity, whichever it may be, the distinctions among the empowerments, the offerings, and the making of requests are all to be done as before.

The only particular difference is expressed in the line: "The maṇḍala of Nairātmyā is to be drawn with two additional elements, beautifully." There is no other difference. Likewise, in placing the emblems of the deities, in the earlier maṇḍala of the principal deity all the emblems in the right hands were set down, whereas here only the curved knife is to be set down. Therefore: "Quickly draw Gaurī and the others. In the east draw the curved knife; so too in the south and west; so too in the north, the fire corner, and the southwest and northwest corners." This is the manner of setting down the emblems of the goddesses. The meaning is that on the two additional elements, one sets down in order the emblems of the goddesses of the object and of the subject. And likewise, by the lines "As was taught for Īśāna, so too below and above," the emblems and so forth of the goddess maṇḍala are well explained.

Now he shows how the vidyā-holding master himself enters the maṇḍala, as follows: "Having made the Vajrasattva posture, the right leg is to be extended; joining both hands in the Hevajra gesture, possessing the dance of compassion through skillful means, the master should enter the maṇḍala." This teaches that the vidyā-holder, through the union of skillful means and wisdom, in which the two are not two, generates equal compassion toward all sentient beings, and, endowed with the postures of the dance of compassion, enters the maṇḍala. Moreover, "bathed, clean, with a fragrant body, and adorned with various ornaments," he does this properly as well. In order to subdue the obstructors of conceptual thought: "Having made the threatening gesture, saying HŪṂ HŪṂ, he terrifies them with HĀ HĀ." Speaking these with his mouth, and performing actions such as entering the maṇḍala, is the definitive practice of the Tathāgatas.

Then the instruction on bringing in the disciple is to be explained. "Afterward the very reality is truly taught: pure wisdom endowed with form, and the self of saṃsāra, which is conceptual thought, have not the slightest difference." This is shown as follows. The vajra master, the vidyā-holder, from the very beginning abides in the recognition that all phenomena are like a dream and like the moon's reflection in water. When he causes the disciple to enter the maṇḍala and performs all the expansive rites, he does not do so with the thought that they are real at that very moment of acting. Why is that? Because all phenomena are, from the very beginning, empty of intrinsic nature. But those who engage in these rites and gather disciples while not free of these perceptions are like a mad elephant: they destroy their own ranks and thereby shatter the other's ranks as well.

In this way, through the distinctive meaning of what is expressed, the special qualities of the mantra-holders who enter the yoga tantras and the action tantras are also shown here. Here, in the great yoga, the distinction is taught to be that of the innate meditative absorption, which makes it superior. Here the word "innate" is understood in two ways. First, for the sake of ordinary beings to realize it, it is shown as the moment of bliss of the two organs. Second, the one who, abandoning the moment of bliss of the two organs, abides in the great innate, is the great vidyā-holding master. With this in mind it is said: "In the supreme delight there is no meditation and no meditator; there is no body, no apprehended and no apprehender; there is no flesh and no blood, no excrement and no urine; no vomiting, no revulsion, no bewilderment, no cleanliness, no offering, no obstruction; no attachment, no aversion, no delusion, no envy; no miserliness, no pride, nothing to be seen; no meditation and no meditator, no friend and no enemy; the innate, which is free of arrogance, is of manifold nature."

This statement, which shows the distinctive meaning of the great yoga, teaches the following. The innate is not merely the bliss of the seminal fluid, reaching the vajra jewel, in the meditative absorption characterized by the "supreme" and the other joys. That was taught for the sake of ordinary beings to realize. The state of the vidyā-holder is this: when the yogin has turned away from the perceptions of body and so on, from apprehended and apprehender; when, even if pierced and cut by material vajras and the like, he is not harmed by the injuries of flesh and blood; when there is no excrement or urine; when there is no vomit, phlegm, watery discharge, mucus or bile, which do not arise; when he moves and speaks unobstructed anywhere; and when no conceptions of the six victors, of the families beginning with attachment, enter him. This itself is definitively taught as the distinction of the vidyā-holder, and it is also taught as the innate that is free of all arrogance. Moreover, the mantra-holder pours this very teaching into the ears of the supreme disciples. Why? Because it is the nature that abides by its own essence. For a phenomenon that abides by its own essence is not something the Tathāgatas themselves would use to alter or change.

Vajragarbha spoke as follows.

Why is it that the body, whose nature is pure and has been without intrinsic nature from the very beginning, is said to have the nature of the elements?

The meaning is this. If the aggregates, the constituents, and so forth are pure by their own nature from the beginning, why are they presented as the elements, and as the gods that arise from the elements, and as the action of karma and of the sense faculties? This does not accord with the way of the nature of reality. That is the sense of the question.

Likewise, in the tantra called the Empowerment of Magical Display it is said:

The buddhas have no characteristics and abide fully in the body of truth. When you teach the Dharma as signless, unconditioned, and without equal, great hero, why do you reveal this ritual with characteristics, the practice of secret mantra? This is not the way of the nature of reality.

When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One, the great Vairocana, then said to Vajrapāṇi: Listen to this characteristic of the Dharma. This Dharma is free of thought and has abandoned all conceptualization. In it, the conceptual thinking of mind and mental factors is completely abandoned. The Dharma that I awakened to in the past arose from the wise. It is not known by children, who range in the domain of wrong conceptualization. Those veiled by darkness hold to place, time, characteristics, and the like. It is in order to take hold of those people that this ritual too is taught. There is no place, and there is no time. There is no action and no agent. In all phenomena there is nothing that exists or does not exist. Phenomena are only suchness.

So it was taught. The passage up to "this is not the way" is the quotation of the question, and the passage beginning "when he had spoken thus" should be understood as the quotation of the reply.

Then the Blessed One, the Vajra-holder, who bestows the bliss of the ḍākinīs, spoke. This indicates that he gave Vajragarbha the answer.

The nature free of pride and conceit abides entirely in the body.

The meaning is this. O Vajragarbha, what I taught to children by elaborating the form of semen, its channels, the winds, and their channels, is precisely, for the wise teachers who hold the vidyā mantras, something that has abided as utterly pure from the beginning. But children and ordinary beings abide bound only by the perception of characteristics.

O Blessed One, why are the aggregates the great elements? This repeats the earlier question: O Blessed One, since all phenomena are pure from the beginning, what is the purpose of teaching the great elements to those who practice secret mantra?

The Blessed One declared:

From the union of bo, la, ka, and kko, the earth of the Dharma, the solidity of touch, comes into being. Because the awakening mind is fluid, the element of water comes into being. From movement, heat is born. Because it moves, it is known as wind. Because of bliss, there is the element of space.

The meaning is this. O Vajragarbha, although all phenomena are pure in this way from the beginning, nevertheless on the conventional level they abide as dependable phenomena of cause and effect. When the two are joined in the way taught for the children, that is, when body meets body and so forth, the earth abides as solid, and from earth water is born. For the awakening mind itself is water. Because heat arises from stirring and movement, there is fire. Because fire and semen move, there is wind. And because in this union bliss free of conceptual thought arises, there is space. That is the meaning.

They abide fully in the five. At the time of the union and so forth, they arise in this way as the nature of the five elements, and therefore they are the great elements. That is the meaning.

Because it is the great element of bliss, that bliss is not itself the real. In this way, through the force of effort the five great elements arise in sequence, and because bliss comes about through them, this is worldly conventional bliss and not the true innate. That is the meaning.

What then? The text says: That which is born together with the innate should be called the innate. It is said to be the innate by nature. For only what transcends all action and conduct, what abides in its own nature, and what has the character of space is the innate, since it transcends all austerities and difficult practices and abides in its own nature. That is the meaning. The root tantra also says:

What abides in its own essential nature is itself the innate. It has the character of going beyond letters and transcends the path of speech.

All aspects are one in restraint. Because the performance of the mudrā is abandoned, for the yogī compassion becomes the means. The inseparability of emptiness and compassion should be called the awakening mind.

The meaning is as follows. It is the single nature of the vow of all the buddhas. It is free of the activity that is the cause of karma and wisdom. For the yogī, compassion is the means, and the bliss of the awakening mind flows without interruption. And that profound way, in which emptiness free of any gem-like substance and compassion are not different, is nondual. This is called the ultimate awakening mind, and should also be called the great innate wisdom.

What is made manifest? The tantra says: There is no mantra recitation, no austerity, no fire offering. There is no maṇḍala dweller, and there is no maṇḍala. That itself is the mantra recitation, the austerity, and the fire offering. That itself is the maṇḍala dweller, and that itself is the maṇḍala. In short, it has the nature in which the mind is gathered.

Here, recitation is so called because all instants are counted as exhausted in the suchness of all the buddhas. Austerity is so called because one abandons the eight worldly concerns and the like by way of non-abandoning, without clinging to them. The fire offering is so called because words, letters, the movements of the mind, and so forth are burned. The maṇḍala dweller and the maṇḍala are so called because they abide in the heart of all that moves and does not move. In this way, this conduct, gathering the mind into one, is taught as the innate.

This is the tenth chapter, the extensive explanation of empowerment, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Hevajra.

Of the nature of Trailokyavijaya, with one face and two arms, bowing down. The extensive explanation of the chapter on the gazes will be given by Vajragarbha.

In this age, when strife flourishes, many beings who are not holy will arise. Seeing no other way to enter them than to subdue them with a wrathful appearance and with fierce looks, the Lord manifested in forms such as the one with one face and two arms, such as Trailokyavijaya, such as Hala-hala, such as Hayagrīva, and such as Yamāntaka. For this reason the text teaches the distinctions of gaze and so on, the distinctions of breath, the postures, the rosary, the hearth, the substances to be burned, and the distinctions of time, such as midnight. Of these, only the gazes and the distinctions of the winds are taught here. The distinctions of time, hearth, and so on should be known from the context of the fire offering and from other extensive tantras.

Concerning the words "One with an uneven forehead is said always to bring about a fall," and so on: dependent origination, the arising of things in dependence, is the defining mark of phenomena, and the Tathāgatas taught it in this and that tantra. Therefore, if a yogin who is intent on the practice of subduing a wicked being transforms himself into a body of the color of Akṣobhya, with a gaze directed upward, and meditates, recites, and offers the fire offering while looking with both eyes at the same time toward the forehead, he will cause a fall. This is because above, in that direction, resides the form of the family of aversion.

Concerning the words "The gaze of the powerful one abides on the left; on the left are the two images": a yogin intent on the practice that brings the three worlds under control transforms himself into the form of Padmarāga, the ruby, and meditates and so on while looking with both eyes at the same time toward the left. By doing so he brings beings under his control. This is because on the left resides a form like Padmarāga.

Concerning the words "Summoning on the right side; and from above, the two images": likewise, a yogin intent on summoning the three realms of beings transforms himself into a form like the rising sun, and meditates and so on while looking slightly toward the upper right with both eyes at the same time. By doing so he summons the three realms of beings. This is because on the right side resides a form like the rising sun.

Concerning the words "The gaze that stiffens, in the center; the two at the center of the tip of the nose": likewise, a yogin intent on paralyzing the three worlds transforms himself into a form like crushed gold, and meditates and so on while looking with both eyes at the same time at the center of the tip of the nose. By doing so he paralyzes the three worlds. This is because the body of the Great Powerful One, like the color of gold, resides in the center.

Now, in order to show the wind to be employed at the time of the four gazes, the text says: "By the exhaling, he causes a fall." At the time of that very gaze, one should apply oneself to the practice as one's own life-breath goes out, as the wind is breathed outward. This is because when the wind is expelled, all embodied beings come to the time of death.

"By the inhaling he brings under control." At the time of that very gaze, one should apply oneself to the practice as one draws one's own life-wind inward. This is because when the life-wind abides within, everything is gathered under one's control.

"By the filling he summons." At the time of that very gaze, one should apply oneself to the practice by not releasing one's own life-wind outward, but filling it well within and holding it by the practice of the vase breath. This is because, with the wind remaining within and nothing going out, the stream of nectar of all embodied beings, like the moon, is drawn in.

"By the peaceful he paralyzes." At the time of that very gaze, one should apply oneself to the practice by neither extending the wind outward or inward, nor drawing it in, but leaving one's own life-wind just as it is. This is because the life-winds of all beings, freed from movement, become paralyzed.

Now, in order to show the measure of the yogin's accomplishment, the text says, "Upon a moist tree he causes a fall," and so on. To master these four gazes, one meditates, recites, and offers the fire offering and so on according to the rite, and accomplishes them. First, signs of experience arise with regard to inanimate substances, and the samādhi is said to become stable. Therefore the text says, "Upon a moist tree he causes a fall," and so on. Having become practiced in the application of killing, the yogin first, as the wind goes out, looks with the gaze of killing at a tree that is moist and sits there with branches and leaves. When it dries up, then, at that very time, he should apply it to the unruly one as well. This is the meaning.

"The powerful one is renowned for flowers." Likewise, when one possessed of the gaze and wind looks at a flower and it dries up, one should apply it. This is the meaning.

"Summoning upon the vajra tree." Likewise, when one possessed of the gaze and the wind looks at a very hard and rough tree called the vajra tree, and it bends down like a well-ripened ear of grain, one should apply it. This is the meaning.

"Paralyzing what is moving and unsteady." Likewise, when one possessed of the gaze and the wind looks at the peaks of high hills, mountains, and the like, and at the finest, softest blades of grass growing there, so delicate that a mere horsehair's tip of wind sets them swaying greatly, and they no longer sway in the wind, one should apply it. This is the meaning.

The definite point here is what the root tantra says. The yogin who possesses the life-wind together with the awakening mind is able to kill with his own body. Likewise, when both life-winds are under his control, he is able to summon his own awakening mind as it is. And likewise, when he has paralyzed both life-winds and they do not move, he turns them like a wheel upon the outer and inner self. Otherwise, it would become a laughingstock to the worldly, and so it is definite that this should not be done.

Now, in order to show the time for accomplishing the union, it is said: "By the union practiced for six months, accomplishment will come about; of this there is no doubt. Do not be deluded about this, for the miraculous power of the buddhas is beyond thought." This means that if one remains without interruption for six months, and, having settled at the limit, acts with the six limbs according to the instruction of the master, one will succeed. This expression "six months" is taught as a common principle of all the tantras. Therefore those who first accomplish the four kinds of activity within their own body, and so tame beings, act in accordance with what is definitive according to the Tathāgatas; hence one should abandon delusion and doubt about this. That is the meaning.

"Having accomplished the four glances, the wise one should subdue beings. These should not be killed, for the pledge would certainly be broken." The meaning is this: the actions such as killing that are taught are only meant for the time being to frighten beings, in order to tame them, and through that they may also be drawn in. Otherwise, if one were truly and definitively to kill, the pledge of the great seal would be broken and one would go to the Avīci hell. Therefore, as the definitive meaning, the four kinds of activity are taught to be accomplished within one's own body. And so it says: "Having abandoned deceiving beings, one may do all that is not to be done. But by merely harming beings, the accomplishment of the seal will not be found." That is, to say nothing of killing and the like, even merely taking up and remaining in some means of deceiving anyone deceives all beings; through that the pledge is broken and one falls away from the accomplishment of the seal endowed with the supreme of all aspects. That is the meaning.

Now, in order to teach the pledges concerning eating, guarding, and so on for practitioners: "There one should eat the pledge substances: the na, the ga, the ha at first, the final śva, and the first śva. For the accomplishment of Hevajra, eat likewise the five nectars." Those who abide in the pledge of Hevajra should eat and keep these, distinguishing outer and inner. Here na is the first letter of the word for man; ga is the first letter of the word for cow; ha is the first letter of the word for elephant; śva is the final letter in the word for horse; and likewise śva is the first letter of the word for dog. Having made these substances equal in measure, one forms a pill the size of a thumb joint, purifies it, multiplies it, and blazes it, and, making it nectar, eats it; this becomes the outer accomplishment. Likewise, inwardly, it is taught that the senses such as the eyes, which have the names "head" and so on, are turned back from their objects and made to abide; thus the supreme accomplishment comes about. Likewise the five nectars, namely bi, mu, ma, ra, and śu, are also to be used outwardly and inwardly; this is the Tathāgata's command.

"The one endowed with the characteristics taught in the Hevajra: it is marked out from the seventh birth. In particular, delighting in reproach, the seventh time brings accomplishment." This shows the provisional and definitive meanings openly. However, in order to make clear what has not been shown, namely the characteristics of one in her seventh birth, it is said: "A pleasing voice and eyes, a body of fragrance, great radiance; her shadow becomes seven. Seeing this, the yogī recognizes her." That is, whichever woman has a lovely voice, long eyes with the white and dark clearly distinguished, a body like musk and a fragrance like camphor, great radiance, and a shadow that becomes seven, is to be recognized and understood as one in her seventh birth. Having found such a one, the yogī forms a pill the size of a mustard seed and eats it, and so moves in the sky and seeks for countless eons. By such a body of karmic maturation, even those who committed the sins of immediate retribution, and those who have fallen from the special bliss of the dharmadhātu, are said to be purified by that very body. Therefore it says: "Merely by eating it, in an instant one becomes a sky-goer."

Now, to describe the accomplishment of the goddess made as the family: "I shall explain the means of accomplishment of Kurukullā," and so on. This too is taught in the root tantra: "Beings of the three existences, deluded by attachment, to subdue them, the Blessed Hevajra brought the world under his power in the form of a woman." The Blessed One himself showed his body in the manner of passionate red and so drew beings in. "Whatever was taught at length in the twelve chapters is gathered here in brief." Here the protection wheel, the celestial palace, the seven hanging ornaments, the generation from the five wisdoms, and so on rest on being accomplished just as they were taught earlier, at the time of accomplishing Hevajra himself. Here it should be understood that only the seed syllable, color, hand emblems, and seat of the goddess are shown: "The goddess arising from the syllable HRĪḤ, red in color, four-armed, holding in her hands arrow and bow, likewise an utpala and an iron hook. Merely by meditating on her, one brings the three worlds under one's power."

Specifically, the seed syllable that generates Hevajra is HŪṂ, and likewise the syllable that generates Nairātmyā and the others is OṂ; here, however, she is generated from HRĪḤ and her very nature is to utter the sound HRĪḤ from her mouth. First, from the syllable HRĪḤ transformed entirely, there is an utpala; into it the Tathāgatas are dissolved; from this arises the red goddess in a half-seated posture, four-armed. In the first two hands she holds the bow and arrow of utpala flowers, and in the others an utpala and an iron hook. Her body is adorned with a garland of half a hundred human heads, her crown ornament has five segments with a topknot and a crescent moon, and her red hair stands upward. Having visualized her thus, and with attention joined with recitation of OṂ KURUKULLE HRĪḤ SVĀHĀ, one will succeed in six months. If one recites according to the rite for a hundred thousand of the syllables' count, it will certainly bring about liberation, and one should perform the ten fire offerings.

As for these rites when done for ordinary purposes, it is as follows. With one hundred thousand repetitions one brings kings under one's power; with ten thousand, ordinary worldly people; with ten million, cattle, yakṣas and the like; with seven hundred thousand, the asuras; with two hundred thousand, the gods themselves; and with one hundred thousand, the holders of mantra. The words "brings under one's power" are to be joined to every line. In every case the fire offering is to be made at one tenth of the number of recitations. If one practices endowed with the gaze and with the conduct that is allied to the vital breath, one succeeds; otherwise one becomes a laughingstock to worldly people.

Here, "Ku" is great bliss. "Ru" is the complete enjoyment. "Ku" is the Dharma. And "Lle" is to be understood as the emanation. By the stages thus taught, one will tame beings.

This is also said in the tantra called the Accomplishment of the Wisdom of Higher Knowledge of the Lotus Family: "Having aroused passion as higher knowledge, and having meditated in perfect equipoise, one becomes master of the five higher knowledges and attains pure accomplishment. So spoke the Blessed Vajra-Holder." Likewise, in the tantra called the Accomplishment of the Wisdom of Great Awakening of the Lotus Family, it is said: "Lord of the World, dwelling in samādhi, through the Dharma mudrā, if one meditates on this wisdom of great awakening, one attains accomplishment. So spoke the Blessed Avalokiteśvara." Therefore yogins who are devoted to taming beings should practice in reliance on this goddess.

In the phrase "the Ḍākinī Network of Vows of Hevajra," the words "Hevajra" and "vajra" have been explained at length above. "Ḍākinī" is the name given to one who possesses a samādhi like space. "Network" means an assemblage, applied to those who are many.

As for "the chapter on the manifest awakening of Vajragarbha": the five hundred thousand verses of the root tantra of Hevajra rest on two chapters, first the chapter requested by Vajragarbha, and afterwards the chapter requested by Nairātmyā. This abridged tantra too is to be understood in that way. Up to this point, the intention is largely the request made by Vajragarbha, but it does not actually rest on Vajragarbha. For Vajragarbha appears and is hidden as the gatherer of the great secret of all the Tathāgatas of the three times, and he is also taught and prophesied as the Lord of Secrets. Therefore these words refer to the majority of the text. And in the second chapter there is nothing that was not requested.

Here ends the extensive commentary on the definitive meaning of Hevajra, namely the explanation of the gaze, and the explanation of the accomplishment of Kuru Kulle, which is the commentary on the eleventh chapter, together with the complete presentation, without omission, of the whole extensive commentary on the first chapter, called the manifest awakening of Vajragarbha.

You who have no body of emptiness and yet possess a body like a vajra, Hevajra, I pay homage to you. Seeing these three worlds oppressed by the thick darkness of ignorance, my heart melts. Therefore Vajragarbha writes the extensive explanation of the chapter on consecration.
