Therefore abandon the error of the thirty-two and the sixteenth; otherwise, do not doubt.

Vajragarbha asked:

Arisen from all the yoginīs,
of the nature of innate joy,
the supreme drink, undiminishing, space itself:
why should camphor not be certainly abandoned?

Therefore the meaning is this: "I shall practice so that this camphor-like substance is never abandoned, for the sake of cooling body, speech and mind with the unbroken bliss like space, the supreme drink, the seminal fluid, the awakening mind, the nature of the fifteen yoginīs."

The Blessed One answered in accord with the proper manner, saying, "It is just as you have said." Then Vajragarbha asked, "By what means should one generate the mind of awakening?" The meaning of this is: how should one train in the sequence of entering this kind of path to awakening?

The Blessed One declared:

By the means of the maṇḍala wheel,
And by the stages of self-blessing,
The awakening mind that has form,
Conventional and ultimate, is fully generated.

This was spoken again to teach the following. First one takes the common vows, then is led into the colored-powder maṇḍala, and the master blesses one with the sevenfold procedure known as the vajra master's empowerment. Only then does one generate the awakening mind with the white conventional substance, and with the bliss that arises from it. That is the meaning.

It is further stated:

The conventional is just like the kunda flower;
The ultimate is that which has the form of bliss.

Here, first, the seminal fluid itself is the conventional awakening mind, while the bliss that depends on it is designated by the convention "ultimate." Later, the true reality that arises from the bliss that is not emitted is the ultimate form, and this is the definitive meaning.

The woman, whose lotus holds bliss,
Is by nature the form of E and VAM.
Because bliss is guarded,
She is proclaimed to be "the blissful one."

Here, conventionally, bliss arises from the mutual friction of the woman's lotus and the man's organ, and so she is proclaimed "the blissful one." That same bliss, bound by the iron chain of reality, gives rise to the attainment of a vajra-like body. This is the definitive meaning. In this very Compendium Tantra it is said:

This very place of buddhas, bodhisattvas,
And the holders of the vajra
Is itself called saṃsāra,
And is itself nirvāṇa.
Having abandoned saṃsāra, elsewhere
Nirvāṇa will not be realized.

By generating the support of a youth and a maiden, like the kunda flower mentioned above, there is the statement: "Saṃsāra is form, sound, and so forth; saṃsāra is feeling and so forth; saṃsāra is the sense faculties themselves; saṃsāra is aversion and so forth." But when one has fully defined the support of youth and maiden, like the kunda flower, then through guarding, these phenomena are not said to be nirvāṇa. And the line "Having abandoned saṃsāra, elsewhere nirvāṇa will not be realized" is itself the definitive meaning.

Therefore:

Through bewilderment, it is the form of saṃsāra.

For the bewildered, the messenger of time seizes them, and the fire of time blazes, so that looking downward the kunda-like substance destroys. Then:

Without bewilderment, by purifying saṃsāra,
Saṃsāra becomes nirvāṇa.

Here, when the kunda-like substance, whose movement has been stopped, is free of bewilderment, it is reversed from the course of dependent origination at the navel, the heart, the throat, and the forehead. Dissolving from place to place, it is said, "Saṃsāra is nirvāṇa." For this reason too it is taught:

The awakening mind is nirvāṇa,
Possessing the manner of the conventional and the ultimate.

Then:

Adorned with beauty and the bloom of youth,
Blue, gentle, of excellent lineage,
The place where camphor arises is Siṃhala;
By oneself, Hevajra bestows the empowerment.

These and similar lines designate the conventional woman, who brings about the realization of bliss, by the convention "mudrā," and this was explained at length earlier. Likewise, her liquor is the innate, and drinking it for the sake of oneself and others is itself the brief teaching. Drinking has two kinds of intent, the provisional and the definitive. In the provisional sense, one actually takes it with flavored substance; in the definitive sense, one takes it through the flavor of the organ. Hence:

Having arranged the bola and kakkola,
The practitioner of the observance goes everywhere.
The camphor that arises from union there
The wise do not abandon.
Therefore it is not taken in the hand,
Nor wiped away like a conch shell with fish.
Certainly, for the sake of insight and strength,
It should be taken with the tongue of the deathless.

Camphor itself is Nairātmyā, bliss, whose manner is selflessness; her bliss is the great mudrā. Because one realizes the culmination of the great mudrā Nairātmyā in dependence on the fifteen camphor drops of the bindu, she is designated "Nairātmyā." In the navel maṇḍala of embodied beings dwells the first of the vowels, in the manner of A. From it, at the time of union, the fire of time blazes and burns all three realms. It also burns all the buddhas dwelling in the ten directions and brings them back to their own place. This is the cause of everything. For this reason it is said:

Dwelling in the navel maṇḍala,
Of the nature of the first vowel,
It is conceived by the buddhas as "insight."

And the root tantra also says:

When all embodied beings unite,
The acts of going and coming,
And all happiness and suffering,
Are said to be done by this alone.

This is a common teaching. Of these, "insight" (blo) is a particular name for wisdom (prajñā). Because it performs the work of piercing and cutting each thing separately, the buddhas have called it wisdom. In the yoga of the completion stage:

That very one is the Blessed Lady Wisdom.

Here, when one passes beyond what is like the world and the mind enters within, that is the place of no transference. From there it blazes and burns all realization of signs. As it is said:

"Penetrating wisdom," so called,
Is known as meditative absorption;
Through it the mudrā is accomplished,
And if meditated upon, it is swiftly accomplished,
So spoke the Blessed Lady Supreme Wisdom.

Thus, one who is free of the elaboration of signs in the beginning, the end, and the middle is Nairātmyā. She is spoken of thus:

She is neither long nor short,
Neither square nor round,
Neither smell, nor taste, nor nirvāṇa;
She performs the work of innate joy.

"She" is to be joined with the line "Of the nature of the first vowel." The meaning is that she is the agent of all that is worldly and beyond the world.

In the same way, she is also the agent of what is understood in many ways as primal nature, the person, and so forth. For instance, the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), whose discernment is confused, say:

Earth, water, fire, wind, and space,
Time, direction, self, and mind:
Substance is of the nature of these nine,
And all things have this as their nature.

And likewise:

Substance, quality, and action,
The universal, the particular, and inherence,
These are the six categories of meaning,
And from them this world arises.

Thus they assign many designations.

Now the verse says: "The yoginī born from that, the very enjoyer of her bliss; together with her, the great mudrā becomes the accomplishment that bestows bliss." The sense of what is said here is this: she enjoys the bliss generated by the first vowel, and she realizes the great mudrā, the bliss arising from that and from each of the vowels, and she also bestows that way upon others.

How is she to give it? The verse answers: "Form, sound, likewise smell, taste, and likewise touch, and the nature of the dharmadhātu as well, are to be examined by wisdom itself." By this the giving is done. It is distinguished as outer and inner. Outwardly, one gives in the manner of gathering together the bliss of the woman. Inwardly, one gives through the power of the vowels and consonants (āli-kāli). This too is stated in this very text: "By the union of the vowels and consonants, the dualities of the outer are barred."

The lines beginning "The form born together with that, the yoginī of excellent great bliss," and those beginning "Having the nature of the dharmadhātu," mean that the mandala wheel, the fire pit, the binding of mudrās, and all that is designated by the worldly and the transcendent conventions are founded on that very same ground, for gods, asuras, pretas, hell beings, insects, dust and flakes, and everything else.

Now, having first taught the outer vows at length, among the outer and inner vows, the text teaches the special inner vow of bliss. Vajragarbha asked the Blessed One: "Having fully understood the path of meditating on the wheel and how all the outer and inner deities arise, please also explain the vow to me." The meaning of this request is that he asked for the inner vow of bliss, in which the outer conduct of discipline is wholly set aside. The Blessed One said:

In the center of the yoginī's body
The vow dwells in the form of A.
As it is outwardly, so it is inwardly;
That very vow is fully taught.

The meaning here is this. Wearing a body wrapped in outer cloth, with the robes cleanly dyed by the impure dye of the dyers and the like, and likewise the four defeats, the thirteen remainders that depend on the Saṅgha and are so worded, the indeterminate offenses, and whatever else is taught in this way, were spoken to guide childish people. Apart from these, even in the practice of conduct within the path of secret mantra itself, the teaching of provisional and definitive meaning is extensively given. From that text come the lines "For the emanation is the elder," through "The place of birth becomes free of desire, the hair of the womb is the monk's robe," and also "Naked, with hair and beard cut off, the man reciting mantras is a monk," and the like. These are taught as definitive meaning for yogis of childish mind, who engage in the first stage of the conduct of the mantra door, as distinct from the outer ritual conduct of the śrāvakas. For wise and intelligent yogis, the teaching beginning "Since the emanation is the elder" is of provisional meaning, while the teaching beginning "In the center of the yoginī's body the vow dwells in the form of A" is of definitive meaning.

Moreover, outwardly the vow is not to commit the defeats and the like. Inwardly, in the vow of bliss, because one does not cut the life of the awakening mind, there is the vow of not taking life. Likewise, because bliss is not allowed to be lost, the vow of turning away from unchaste conduct and the other vows are guarded, and this is taught as the definitive meaning. In the Chanting of the Names, too, we find: "Supreme in austerity, of great austerity, one who dwells in purity, Gautama." And likewise: "Supreme holder of great discipline, holder of great patience, steadfast." These and similar lines extensively teach the vow of definitive meaning.

Thus it is said: "The bliss of the bola is the great mudrā, and is all the vajra sense sources." This means that when one enters the path of sun and moon, which belongs to the indestructible path, the outer actions and the mudrā of wisdom and the like are abandoned. Therefore the vow of bliss is fully expanded, and all the channels become like the vajra. This is the nature of the secret absorption. Hence it is said: "By this secret absorption the dualities of the outer are barred."

Furthermore, to refute the idea held by most childish people that the "three bodies" are something external, it is taught: "The three bodies are rightly explained as the form of the wheel within the body." For in the wheel of great bliss and the rest, the dharmakāya, the saṃbhogakāya, and the nirmāṇakāya, all-pervaded by the three channels, are taught as the three bodies, once they are fully understood. Why? Because it is said: "Lalanā is called the body; Rasanā is speech; and when Avadhūtī abides in the center, the freedom from grasped and grasper is the dharmakāya." Likewise, because great bliss pervades everything.

And further: "From where do all beings dispute their birth? Because the emanation is the elder, therefore it is the nirmāṇakāya." For when the body of every person forms, it forms first from the navel, and the life wind and the rest are also emanated from there. So the navel is called "the elder" and is the emanation wheel. Why? Because it is said: "The body that generates and emanates is called the emanation body."

As for "The nature of mind is Dharma, and the dharmakāya is also in the heart," the meaning should be understood from the saying: "When one holds all pleasure and pain, the sign becomes signless from the sign. The signless, unconditioned dharmakāya dwells in the place of the heart."

Of the six tastes and the embodied beings who partake of them, it is said that their foods are surely enjoyed here. This is because the path of the complete supply of food and drink, six tastes and the rest, and the channel that grasps those tastes, lie in the throat. Therefore it is said: in the throat is the wheel of enjoyment. And in the crown the great bliss abides. This means it abides in the basis of bliss.

Now, to speak of the result: Evaṃ, in its aspect, is just the cause in accord. As one has acted, so one experiences. This is proclaimed as "the result in accord with the cause." The meaning is this: in the place of the navel wheel, Evaṃ, the fire of time blazes; it travels along the right channel to the place of bliss, and it draws the flow and sends it to that very place, so that it is experienced there. As for the ripened result, from the wheel of Dharma: because the action is small and the result great, it is the reverse of that ripening. That is to say, the result to be ripened by fire comes by way of the channel of water. As for the result that is the action of a person, enjoyment: it is the producing that acts as a person. The meaning is that, although such causes and results have their own functions, they depend on the exertion of a person. The wheel of great bliss is utterly stainless; the yogī is pure and without stain. This is said because it is given by that which is called "the place that is stainless in its continuum." Therefore: the fruit, distinguished as the result in accord with the cause and the rest, is declared to be of four kinds. Urged on by the wind of karma, the Blessed One engages Prajñā in action. The wind of karma that abides by nature in the navel wheel is called Prajñā; and it is this wind of karma that kindles the fire of time and burns all the world systems.

Now I shall show the definitive explanation of the four great schools. As for "because the emanation is the Elder (Sthavira)," the meaning is that, abiding there, it steadies the basis for the life-breath's going and coming. As for "because speech of the Dharma arises, all the wheels of Dharma exist," it means that the sound of the life-wind in the heart makes all that is spoken clear. As for "because all are honored in the throat, the wheel of enjoyment is honored by all": the foods that nourish all the channels, and all the tastes of drink, travel through the throat; therefore, since it is honored by all the channels, it is said in this way. As for "because the great bliss abides in the head, the great assembly in the wheel of bliss," this is because the awakening mind, like jasmine, for the most part abides in the wheel of bliss. As for "the school is said to be the body, and the belly should be spoken of as the dwelling": the eighteen schools are commonly spoken of as the whole body, and the mother's womb is spoken of as the monastery. Likewise, the mantra recitation is A and Hūṃ. The womb-source, the form of the wheel, is A; and the form of great bliss is Hūṃ. The meaning is that from the navel wheel the sound Aṃ, the life-wind, touches the Hūṃ of the wheel of the thirty-two; therefore it is said in this way. Hence A-haṃ means: "I am, from the beginning, of the nature of the body of great bliss."

By the conditions of these, sentient beings are without doubt buddhas. The ten months are themselves the grounds; the sentient being is lord of the ten grounds. Here, the ten winds, root and branch, of provisional and definitive meaning, are loosened; by definitive meaning they are ten.

Then, from "the yoginī, Nairātmyā and all the goddesses" up to "they fell upon the ground, trembling," the meaning is this: the Blessed One taught that all sentient beings are, from the beginning, of the nature of the ten grounds, with the body of a bodhisattva like a vajra. Why, then, do sentient beings suddenly fall into the place of affliction and distress? How could they arise from the mother? Thus the goddesses fainted. Again, from "the Vajra-holder, seeing those goddesses, because he was disturbed" up to "I myself, with delight, will tell you that very thing, listen," this shows the sound of the song that the Blessed One uttered to make those goddesses joyful. Hearing the speech of the Blessed One as if in a dream, all those goddesses regained their life. That is the meaning.

The Blessed One said: sentient beings are buddhas, yet they are suddenly obscured by stains; when these are removed, they are buddhas. Since all sentient beings are from the beginning of the nature of co-emergence, they are buddhas. The sudden stain is a leak; the severing of its cause is what is meant by "buddha."

The goddesses said: Blessed One, it is so, it is so; there is nothing true and nothing false. What was taught, that the leaking is the stain and the non-leaking is the buddha, is indeed so; this is the meaning of "it is so, it is so."

And the Blessed One himself gave this example: one who does not know, an ordinary person, faints from eating strong poison. Having abandoned bewilderment, one fully cuts through its reality by the mind that knows it. For example, some deluded people, of poor skill in the means of turning poison to account, eat poison and thereby die. But one who eats while fully knowing the reality of the poison finds the poison equal to nectar. In the same way: knowing the means of peace, if one draws breath in Kye's Vajra, one is not held by ignorance and the rest, nor bound by delusion and the rest. That is, freed from the going and coming of the wind that moves in the life-force, and when the drop like jasmine arises and the entry is fully pressed, one draws breath in Hevajra, and is not bound by any affliction such as ignorance. This is the definitive meaning. From their own realization and that of others, there is not a single sentient being who is not a buddha.

Hell beings, hungry ghosts and animals, gods, demigods and humans, worms in excrement and the rest, all have a nature of constant bliss.

This means that the bliss common to all living creatures, the bliss of the bindu that depends on the vital activity of life, as in the crab and the sea monster, is realized just as each one has it. Then the buddha and the bodhisattva are none other than oneself. That is the meaning.

Gods and demigods too, because they do not know that bliss, are bound in saṃsāra by that very ignorance; this is how the line is to be connected. Their nature is constant bliss. In no world-realm anywhere will a buddha be found elsewhere. This is to be connected thus: having abandoned the bliss of self-awareness, one will not find a buddha anywhere else.

What was taught in the manner of a summary is this: the mind is itself the perfect buddha, and so on, down to: those who seek killing as their aim, having known that Hevajra, will attain accomplishment; of this there is no doubt. The meaning is that if one knows for oneself, through one's own mind, that Hevajra, whose nature is the inseparability of the awareness of emptiness and compassion, then one will attain accomplishment. That is certain.

The ignorant are obscured by not knowing, and so they do not know the course of this. This means: falling away from the bliss that is the inseparability of emptiness and compassion, they do not know this course of bliss. What is called the six realms, the crab or sea monster, the limit of the six realms, becomes saṃsāra, and so they become deluded.

The compassionate Vajragarbha, having found the method of Hevajra, he who purifies the objects, will attain the unsurpassed. That is to say, through the power of receiving the guru's instruction, one finds the method; and if one purifies the objects in due order according to the guru's instruction, one will attain the unsurpassed. Here is how: in the place of the four wheels, one kills twenty thousand six hundred and one life-forces, namely five thousand four hundred and five thousand four hundred, and one counts these in due order with the joys that arise by way of the sequence, the joy, the supreme joy, the special joy, and the innate joy. In the same way, in the place of the sixty-four, one kills eighty-four winds, each with one foot. Then, as sixteen channels each are killed with their winds, the four joys are again connected in the same way. Likewise, in the place of the Dharma, one kills six hundred and seventy-five, six hundred and seventy-five. Then, as the channels are killed two by two, three hundred and fifty, three hundred and fifty, the four joys are connected as before. Likewise, in the place of enjoyment, one kills three hundred together with their feet, in excess of thirty-seven. Then, as they are killed four parts by four parts, one connects the four joys each time, as above. Then in the place of the thirty-two, one kills the hundreds, each together with its feet, in excess of sixty-eight. Then, as they are killed four parts by four parts, one connects the four joys as above. All that has been stated is purification according to the guru's instruction.

Now I shall explain the reasoning by which each is sealed with which seal. Vajragarbha asked: the earth is said to be Pukkasī; and since delusion is thin, the body is said to be Vairocana; so how, Lord, is Akṣobhya the seal? Putting these and similar doubts to the Blessed One, he received this answer from the Blessed One: having abandoned the body, by the mind alone no beauty arises elsewhere. Therefore the mind is Vairocana, and the body is sealed with the seal of the mind.

This is said because, just as an action is not fully accomplished by wisdom without means, or by means without wisdom, so an action is not fully accomplished by a body that is separate from mind. Therefore the body is sealed with the seal of the mind.

In the same way: the water element is said to be Ḍombī, and Akṣobhya has the form of the seminal fluid. The seal of the chief, Akṣobhya, is fitting to be set upon Ḍombī. Here Vajragarbha asked whether it is not proper that the water element be sealed by the means, Akṣobhya, who is the water element. And the Blessed One again said: having abandoned the mind, the abiding of the body is not seen as anything else. If so, the mind would become delusion.

As above, this is to be understood through the fault of mutual contradiction. Therefore, if the family of mind is sealed by the family of body, it becomes beautiful. That is the meaning.

In the same way: fire is said to be Caṇḍālī, and desire is the seal of Caṇḍālī. If that family were not the other seal, how, Lord, is Ratnasambhava the seal? This means: is it not proper that the fire element, Caṇḍālī, be sealed by the seal of desire, Amitābha? Why then is the family of fire sealed by Ratnasambhava? So Vajragarbha asked, and the Blessed One said this: since desire is said to be blood, and blood is Ratnasambhava himself, and heat, being the nature of blood, is desire, the family of avarice is sealed with the seal.

This was taught because the heat that is the nature of fire is a quality of blood, and without blood there is no heat. Therefore the family of avarice is taught to be sealed by Ratnasambhava.

Moreover: since Yungmo is wind itself, and Amoghasiddhi has the form of wind, the chief goddess, by the seal of Amoghasiddhi, comes to be fittingly sealed upon Yungmo. This means: when Yungmo is said to be wind, is not the seal of wind itself, Amoghasiddhi, fitting? Why then is the family of desire, Amitābha, set as the seal? So Vajragarbha asked, and the Blessed One said this: apart from desire, jealousy does not arise elsewhere. Therefore the wise seal Yungmo with the seal of desire.

Thus all jealousy arises from desire, and since jealousy is not seen apart from the desire of desire, it was taught that the family of Amitābha, desire, seals the family of wind.

The reasonings stated in this way are the reasonings for sealing the goddesses of the four elements, and one should understand that the goddesses of the directions, whose nature is the aggregates, are to be grasped in the same sense. Therefore it is said: since form is solidity itself, and so on, down to the seal arises from what is not reversed.

Having been taught extensively in this way, it should be understood in sequence. Now the reality of the torma is to be shown.

Kye, Vajra, Vajra-holder,
to the deity in union,
for the sake of beings, the great torma.
Concerning this, Nairātmyā made her request.

This means that Nairātmyā made her request concerning the reality of the outer and inner torma to the Blessed One, who abides in the bliss in which emptiness and compassion are inseparable.

Having assumed the posture of Evaṃ,
to guard the lives of beings
from obstructors and misleading spirits,
Vajrasattva taught the torma.

Here the words should be joined in this way: after Nairātmyā had made her request, intending to benefit all the elemental spirits, outer and inner, he spoke this teaching on the reality of the torma.

Concerning this, he spoke the mantra beginning with Oṃ Inda, Yama, Jala, Jakṣa, Bhūta, Bahi, Vāyu, Rākṣa, Canda, Sujja, Māta, Babba, Tāla, Pātāla, Aṭa, sarva svāhā. Its meaning is as stated in the root tantra:

Protecting oneself and the yogic place,
together with the maṇḍala and its dwelling,
generating oneself as Hevajra,
one should perform the rite of the torma.
In the same way, the celestial palace and so forth
are generated in due order.
In the space of the central triangle,
one sets out the spirits, whose nature is Hevajra.
Because their nature is body, speech and mind,
from Oṃ, Āḥ and Hūṃ,
one thinks of all the guests
and focuses on them as body, speech and mind.
Then, in the proper way, with go-ku and so forth,
purified, increased, kindled, and with nectar,
make it complete with the four syllables,
and offer it to the assembly.

Having understood the meaning of this teaching, one should guard one's own life and the lives of others.

In the same way, there is also the definitive meaning. The eight petals of the channels in the heart of each spirit are the eight sessions, beginning with Indra. The Rāhu above and the fire of time below are the sun and the moon. Tāla and Pātāla are the upper and lower parts of the navel. The mantra that blesses them so that they are satisfied is "A kāro mukhaṃ" and so forth. In the definitive meaning, the letter A is the door of all phenomena, because they are unborn from the beginning; this is the blessing. Here, "A" refers to what lies beyond all verbal expression, abiding as the indestructible sound. This indestructible sound is the great life-force. It is certain that this torma guards life, whereas by any other method, even if one gives torma for countless eons, life will not be protected. This is the word of the Tathāgata. The other definitive meanings are well established in the extensive explanation in the chapter on mantra, and so they are not shown clearly here.

Regarding the passage "If one honors all the spirits with this torma, the yogins will prosper," and so forth, it is to be distinguished by provisional and definitive meaning. The provisional meaning is what was taught in the outer manner. The definitive meaning is this. "Ādyanutpannatvāt": the life-wind belonging to the indestructible, by which there is no emission, is bliss; that bliss is stainless bliss. Likewise, the channels of the sun at the end of time are burned, and so the goddesses and those who have attained prosperity are delighted. This is the meaning. "If one constantly gives torma here for the assembly of spirits" means: if one gives torma by means of the indestructible life-wind. Because bliss is gathered under one's power, there is power; because the afflictions of emission are destroyed, there are fierce rites, the destruction of the hosts of enemies, banishing, and killing. Because bliss is attracted, and through it the torment of the afflictions is pacified and the wisdom of bliss is increased, there are pacifying and enriching.

Now the reasoning for sealing with the mudrās of body, speech and mind is to be shown. Vajragarbha made his request:

By what mudrā are the Khecarīs sealed?
By whose mudrā are the Bhūcarīs?
Chief one, I did not understand this before.

This means: by what mudrā should the Khecarīs and the Bhūcarīs be sealed? The Blessed One Hevajra spoke again:

In the center of the wheel of the three secrets,
by the division into body, speech and mind,
the wheel is established in the center,
abiding above, below and in the middle.

Concerning this: the wheel of the three secrets is divided threefold, into underground, ground level and above ground, and is proclaimed in the Cakrasaṃvara wheel. The Bhūcarī has the mudrā of body. The upper face, whose mudrā is of body, has the family of body, which is delusion, and is proclaimed as Vairocana. The Khecarī has the mudrā of desire. The lower face is Vajrā of speech, has the family of speech, which is desire, and is proclaimed as Amitābha. Nairātmyā is the vajra of mind. Her mind has the immeasurable manner; her mind abides in the central place, and therefore Nairātmyā is born in the center. Her mind has the family of space, and the protector Akṣobhya is proclaimed as mind. These three are the form of the central channel. Three years and a half moon, and two: this is taught as its Dharma. The great tantras such as the Guhyasamāja are said to arise from this very thing. Therefore the families of body, speech and mind are declared to be the three-faced one. In the extensive division, the families are said to be six: the elements of earth, water, fire, wind and space, and the element of wisdom, making six. The three are sun, moon and fire. The five are Rāhu, the dark one, and the other seizer. Adding two to three makes five. The king of the wrathful ones is the fearsome Six-Faced, and Mañjuvajra has five faces. This is the meaning.

Furthermore, in the yoga tantras, so that outsiders may enter, this is taught:

Akṣobhya, Vairocana, Ratnasambhava,
Amitābha, Envy, Vajrasattva:
the families are taught as six,
and the six afflictions are purified.
Aversion, delusion, avarice,
desire, envy and bliss
are proclaimed as the six afflictions.
Through this one meditates in stages.
Having abandoned Vajrasattva,
afterwards there are five families.

This means that having abandoned the desire of crowds, they become five families; that is, having abandoned the supremely unchanging wisdom.

Why is this so? Because the family of wisdom pervades all of them. In the same way, by the division into rajas, darkness, and vigor, or by the division into sun, moon, and Rāhu, it is said: "Through attachment, aversion, and delusion, the vajra becomes threefold." That is the meaning.

In the same way, consciousness is this very element of space. It is the essential nature of all the families, or of a single family, because one space abides in forms that are not different from one another. Thus it is said: "Akṣobhya, aversion, the embodied one, is of one taste with the lord of the mind." Therefore, from this very family of the mind arise the five families, the six families, and so on, up to the immeasurable families. For this reason it is said: "This blessing of the vajra of aversion is spoken of as the five families and the six families."

This is the fourth chapter, the extensive explanation that condenses the mudrās of all the tantras, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Eight faces and sixteen hands: having bowed to the hero with the garland of skulls, Vajragarbha writes this extensive explanation of the Hevajra's Abhisamaya. "Sixteen hands, eight faces, four feet, and terrifying, the hero with the garland of skulls is embraced in body by Nairātmyā." Thus it was taught. The sixteen hands are marked by the sixteen emptinesses; the eight faces are the purity of the eight liberations; the four feet are the purity of the four means of gathering disciples, beginning with acting in accord with their aims. He possesses the signs of wrath, namely innate wrath, wrath through force of circumstance, wrath with accompaniments, and wrath by intention. He wears a garland of heads, the purity of the fifty expressers, and is embraced in body by the Great Mother, whose nature is emptiness. The five syllables are the purity of the five mudrās that he holds. This is Heruka, the Blessed One, to whom Nairātmyā put her request: "If you would explain my wheel, which is surrounded by fifteen, then how is your own maṇḍala, O Lord? I have not seen it before." What this means is as follows. My maṇḍala has the nature of the fifteen portions of the moon of awakening mind and is characterized by awakening through the five and the twenty aspects of full realization, and it has been well taught to be of the nature of the goddesses, five and three. How then is your maṇḍala, O chief? It is fitting that you teach what was not taught before.

"Having made a gesture of invitation to Nairātmyā, and having given her the vajra and the skull cup, and having caressed the goddess's breasts, he showed the maṇḍala correctly." The meaning is this: in order to show Nairātmyā his own maṇḍala, he first taught the Dharma of desire, and then agreed to explain.

"The wheel as previously described" and so on, down to "well adorned with vajra lines," is taught. When the characteristics of the maṇḍala palace are distinguished as outer and inner, the nature of generation is like your own palace of stacked tiers. That is the meaning.

"Following upon great desire, from the nature of innate joy, you and I, the lovely one, are together; in the center of that, I am present." Here the meaning is: O goddess, your maṇḍala is of the nature of the fifteen made at the beginning. Here, however, you and I, whose nature is the complete abandonment of the fifteen, are intimately joined, and from this you are surrounded by eight goddesses of innate nature.

From "to the one with eight faces" down to "marked on the crown with a crossed vajra," and from there to "in the delightful city, you and I," the Blessed One, the great chief himself, clearly shows to Nairātmyā the loving nature of his body. In order to draw in childish beings, he shows a body perfect in marks, adorned with all qualities and possessed of all splendor. "In the delightful city, you and I sport greatly with joy." The meaning is that in the maṇḍala palace, whose nature is the dharmadhātu and which is adorned with the qualities of an incomparable realm, they abide, sporting in innate joy.

"Having drawn out Gaurī from the direction of Indra, she abides correctly at the eastern gate," and so on, down to "in the direction of the wind, Yungmo herself," are vajra words showing how eight goddesses arise from the sport of innate bliss. By this the activity of father and mother begetting children is shown, which is the Dharma that causes worldly existence to increase. This same process, by the distinction of the coming and going of the life-breath of embodied beings, comprises four breaths on the right and four on the left, and four sessions and four junctions, eight in all. These lead the drops of seminal fluid, sixteen and a half, and they show the Dharma of day and night. The breath that enters the fiery line at the junction is shown through Nairātmyā and the chief himself.

Then: "From the vajra of great passion, the consort together with him became seminal fluid." This shows that the activity of father and mother has been fully completed, and the mingling is thoroughly displayed. Otherwise, it is shown that all the elements that depend on the channels of the life-breath dissolve inward and no longer move.

In the same way, it is the meaning of the words that he is roused to rise from the ground of the aeon, and that by the two paths he shows the mastery of awakening. "From the offering of various songs, then the goddesses urged him: O noble one, arouse your compassionate mind," and so on, down to "do not cut off your compassion." With these words of the vajra song the meaning is this. In the world, when parents have passed away, sons and daughters and others lament, cry out, and make pleas, saying: "O protector, we have no protector or refuge; please protect us, kyi hud, kyi hud!" and they scatter themselves about. In the same way, when nirvāṇa is shown to be near in the region of the city of Kuśinagara, the great śrāvakas lament, cry out, and make pleas, and the nāga kings also grieve. Yet all the life-breaths of others enter inward and abide in the element of great bliss, and from that state, for the sake of all sentient beings, he is roused by the elements of earth, water, fire, and wind, and engages in the benefit of beings. This is the definitive meaning.

The pot-bellied one, the donkey, the female buffalo, the human, the śarabha, and the cat: in the eight skulls of the right hand, the elephant and the rest should be understood in this order. This shows the eight emblems of the right hands. In the same way: earth and water and wind, fire, the moon and the sun itself, Antaka, and the holder of wealth: these are in the eight skulls of the left. This shows the eight emblems of the left hands. The purification of these is to be understood from the passage that begins with "the lungs and the breath" and ends with "perfectly accomplishing all the wealthy."

In the same way, the great chief is shown endowed with the nine moods of dance. Thus: the seven precious things, and among them what the Sugata proclaimed as the precious general. The general is one endowed with the nine modes. With graceful bearing he brings forth wisdom in beings, and his conduct is without timidity. The wrathful one endowed with ferocity is terrifying; he cuts off wrong views and aversion in the manner of slaughter. For the one who raises up Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata is heroic in valor. For the vajra-holder, compassion; for the boisterous, the supreme Lord of the World. Vajra Sun is greatly wrathful; for the fierce vajra, terror. Śākyamuni is for the repulsive, and Arali is for the wondrous. The marvelous one proclaims the Dharma, appearing laughing. He shows wrath toward the hard to tame as compassion. He accomplishes the afflictions in a wondrous way. In the wrathful and fierce manner, the unmoving, he is in the manner of peace. These and the like are the meaning of the nine dance poses.

The raising up of the hero endowed with such nine dance poses is this: "With OṂ and HŪṂ the great vajra arises from the form of the seminal drop; and as the feet strike the earth, gods and asuras are terrified." This means that from the form of the earlier seed, the two syllables AṂ and HŪṂ become a skull and a curved knife, and from these the form arises. The bodies of Nairātmyā and of the chief are completely perfected: sixteen and twelve, the sun and moon, perfectly clear and free of stain. That he thereby works for the benefit of beings is the definitive meaning.

GAṂ, CAṂ, BAṂ, GHAṂ, PAṂ, ŚAṂ, LAṂ, ḌAṂ: these are the eight initial letters of the names, in the form of drops. "With these the seeds are drawn forth" should be applied at the time of generating the eight goddesses. In the same way, from "the seeds of the lord, AṂ and HŪṂ, blazing blue and terrifying" up to "the chief is to be meditated upon in just this way," the nature of the generation of the chief is clearly shown, as already taught above.

Now, to show the emblems in the right and left hands of the eight goddesses, it is taught from "in Gaurī's right, a curved knife" up to "in the left, a threatening finger, likewise." Thus, upon each one's seat, within the joined sun and moon, one visualizes the earlier letters abiding. When these are transformed, one visualizes them as the eight emblems of the right hands, and from their transformation the eight goddesses are fully perfected. Why are they perfected from the emblems of the right? As the root tantra says: "With the right hand of the embodied, all actions are accomplished. The right hand of emptiness-wisdom likewise performs all actions. From striving for the benefit of beings, with the dance of the half-lotus posture, the goddesses beginning with Gaurī are wished to abide well in every way."

In the same way, the eight emblems of the right hands, the curved knife and the others, are applied in order: cutting off the afflictions; proclaiming the sound of emptiness; unmoving bliss; adorning with the very suchness of aversion and the other afflictions; removing all doubt in every action; relying on the arhat; turning the wheel of Dharma; and destroying the afflictions. Likewise the eight of the left are applied in order: bliss in touching; awakening from delusion; guarding bliss; taking up wisdom; utterly cutting off the afflictions; displaying bliss; being without affliction; and displaying the stainless abode.

"Gaurī and the others are described with two hands, three eyes, hair bound upward, and adorned with the five mudrās." This and the like shows that all the goddesses are inseparable emptiness and compassion, the suchness of body, speech, and mind, and the suchness of the wisdom of bliss, and are alike endowed with the five mudrās beginning with space.

"Gaurī becomes black in color," up to "Yungmo is called the Variegated One": the meaning is that the body colors of the eight goddesses of the directions and intermediate directions should be understood in order, through the qualities of the natures of earth, water, fire, wind, and the rest.

In the same way: "Yama, the lord of wealth, Nairṛti, Varuṇa, the wind god, Brahmā, Indra, and Īśāna, the fierce, are the seats of Gaurī and the rest." These are the eight guardians of the directions; they are to be visualized as seats in order, according to the qualities of their natures. Furthermore, eight kinds of worldly gods who are proud are made to fall prostrate and are subdued. Then Gaṭṭogdma and the others are empowered and named, and they are placed in the worldly maṇḍala. That is the meaning.

These are as follows. Tapaśvinī, the one who turns back austerity, is named Wrathful Vajrā. The Golden One is named Vajra Golden One, and Śirṣyā is named Vajra Youth. Brahmāṇī is named Vajra Peaceful One. Īśvarī is named Vajra Spitting One. These are empowered as Vajra Victorious Lady. Then, to all the queens who move in the sky, it is given as follows. Amarā is named Vajra Immortal. Rohanā is named Vajra Radiant. The staff-bearer is named Vajra Supreme Staff. The one who steals beings is named Vajra Waist-Bound, and these and the others are empowered as wrathful vajras. Then, to all the queens who move in space, it is given as follows. The Slayer is named Vajra Melting One. The Eater is named Vajra Eater. The Armored One is named Vajra Armored Lady. The Joyful One is named Vajra Joyful Lady. These and the others are empowered as vajra messengers. Then, to all the queens who move on the earth, it is given as follows. The Peaceful Lady is named Vajra Messenger. Wind is named Vajra Swift One. Fire is named Vajra Blazing One.

Kuberā is named Vajra Retinue. Those and others are empowered as Vajra Messengers.

Then, to all the consorts who dwell beneath the earth, he gave names as follows. The Sow is named Vajra Mouth. Cāmuṇḍī is named Vajra Time. Nāsikācchedā is named Vajra Putrid. The water goddess is named Vajra Water-Monster. They are empowered as Vajra Maidservants.

In the same way, as follows. Maheśvarī is named Vajra Wrathful Lady. Mahābalā is named Vajra of Illusion. The Young Sound is named Vajra Bell. Brahmā is named Vajra of the Sage. Īśvarī is named Vajra Weapon. They are empowered as the knowledge-kings.

Then, to those who move in the sky, he gave names as follows. The Sun is named Amṛtakuṇḍalin. The Moon is named Vajra Light. Mahāprabhu is named Vajra Striker. The Tawny Purple One is named Vajra Tawny Purple. These and others are empowered as Vajra Wrathful Ones.

Then, to all the gods who move in space, he gave names as follows. The Honey-Intoxicated is named Vajra Elephant-Nose. The Honey-Maker is named Vajra Garland. The Victor is named Vajra Power. The One Who Brings Victory is named Vajra of Complete Victory. These and others are empowered as chiefs of the assembly.

Then, to all the lords of the upper earth, he gave names as follows. The Treasure Keeper is named Vajra Hammer. Fire is named Vajra Fire. Wind is named Vajra Wind. Kubera is named Vajra Terror. These and others are empowered as Vajra Messengers.

Then, to all the gods who are lords of the underworld, he gave names as follows. The Boar is named Vajra Iron Hook. Yama is named Vajra Time. Gaurī, the hair-tufted one, is named Vajra Chief of Obstructors. Varuṇa is named Vajra of the Nāgas. These and others are empowered as those called Vajra Servants. This is for the purpose of subduing all the arrogant ones of the world.

Having spread out the bola, embraced it closely, and made the offering of milk, and having reverently honored the Blessed One, Nairātmyā asked for the mantra. The meaning of these lines is that Nairātmyā, with the taste of bliss, fully honored her lord and asked for the mantra of Hevajra.

In the same way: the mantra that brings women under control, likewise the subduing of the wicked, the subduing of nāgas, and the striking of gods and asuras, this I shall explain. Goddess, giver of bliss, listen. This describes the qualities of the root mantra. Whatever the qualities of the mantra are, I shall explain that by merely reciting it women come under one's control, the wicked are destroyed, nāgas are subdued, and gods and asuras are tamed. This is the meaning.

I have not taught this to others, not to buddhas or bodhisattvas. Whatever arises through this mantra, and whatever Vajrasattva has done with it, I explain to you, fearsome goddess, because you are not a stranger to me. This means that the qualities of this mantra could not be fully told even over countless eons, and that they are not told even to śrāvakas, solitary victors, and those who have newly generated the awakening mind; this secret is told to you alone.

Having raised the maṇḍala, agitated by garlands of flame, in order to give empowerment to Vajragarbha, one should summon the supreme drop. This means: set up the fearsome maṇḍala of Hevajra, which makes even the fearsome greatly afraid, and learn the reality of the maṇḍala from the vajra master. Having recited the mantra one hundred thousand times according to the number of its syllables, one performs the fire offering with the ten requisites. Having first done the approach, and having applied it to the rite with ten thousand recitations, one can summon even a goddess such as the supreme drop; what need is there to speak of ordinary women? This is the meaning, and these are the qualities of the mantra.

Otherwise, in terms of the wheel of great bliss, the summoning of the supreme drop is by stopping the life-wind; this is the definitive meaning. This is also the empowerment of Vajragarbha.

With a long and good sound, joined with the union of Hevajra, one should summon all the consorts. With an undoubting mind, the yogin, by reciting one hundred thousand times through the yoga of Hevajra, accomplishes all its activities. As for the definitive meaning here: when, from the prosperity of the nature whose essence is emptiness and compassion, one holds the life-wind one hundred thousand times, one comes to accomplish all activities. This is definitive.

Regarding this: give the first of the vowels, adorned with the half-moon and the drop. The first of the vowels is the letter A. The half-moon is like the crescent of the second day of the lunar month, and it adorns the head of the A. Above that, again, it is adorned with the drop. This is the meaning.

Oṃ aṣṭānanāya, piṅgalojhrakeśavartmane, caturviṃśatinetrāya, ṣoḍaśabhujāya, kṛṣṇajīmūtavapuṣe, kapālamālānekaṃdhāriṇe, adharmatākrūracittāya, arddhenduvaṃṣṭriṇe. Oṃ māraya māraya, kāraya kāraya, garjaya garjaya, tarjaya tarjaya, śoṣaya śoṣaya, saptasāgarān bandha bandha, nāgāṣṭakān gṛhṇa gṛhṇa, sarvasthūn, ha hā hi hī hu hū he hai ho hau haṃ haḥ phaṭ svāhā. These are the lines of the great root mantra, the mantra from which all qualities arise.

Then, the passage beginning "Then that goddess, delighted, from the practice of rubbing and being rubbed" and ending with "he made the maṇḍala of that very one" should be understood to mean that Nairātmyā requested the maṇḍala from the Blessed One, and that the Blessed One himself taught the maṇḍala.

The passage from "with one stage and four doors" up to "and well endowed with five lines" should be understood as teaching the characteristics of the colored-powder maṇḍala. Likewise, "then draw the eight vases" means that they are drawn at the doors and the intermediate points.

Of powder of the five precious substances, or else of powders of grain and the like; and if not, of the tooth-marks of boars in the charnel ground, and likewise of charnel-ground charcoal. Verses of this kind mean the following. Whoever wishes to draw a maṇḍala of colored powders should draw it with powders of gold, silver, and the like, if these can be had. If they cannot be obtained, he may also draw it with powders of grain, such as rice, dyed in different colors. If even that is not possible, it is certain that he draws with the red colored powder made from the boar-rooted earth of the charnel ground, the black made from the charcoal of that same place, and the white made from human bone.

Then the text says: In its center draw a lotus of eight petals with its stamens, and in the middle a human skull, white in color, of three parts. The meaning is this: in the center of a lotus of eight petals, complete with anthers and filaments, one draws a skull of three fragments.

In the same way, the passage that runs from "in the northeast, a śarabha" up to "filled with the rice that grows in the water" shows that the eight emblems on the right are to be drawn, as is to be understood from what was said earlier. These should also be drawn according to the natural qualities of each, with the emblems of the goddesses matching the colors of the goddesses.

For the extended ritual of the maṇḍala, one should consult the great tantra called the Saṃpuṭa; here only the vital points have been taught. That is the meaning. For what is the use of saying more here? It is shown by the line, "Just as in the Compendium of Reality, so one should perform the maṇḍala ritual."

In the same way, the age of the consorts of great bliss is stated as follows: the eight consorts of great bliss, aged twelve and eight and two, adorned with necklaces and anklets, should be brought into the maṇḍala. This is explained thus. At twelve years they are utterly pure, the vajra sun of great radiance. At sixteen years they are utterly pure, the sixteenth part of the moon, the vajra moon of stainless light, free of passion and of great passion in the first. By this reckoning of sixteen in halves, the consorts are explained as eight. They are known as adorned with the drops of bliss, as necklaces and anklets. The passage running from "mother and sister" to "likewise the father's sister, the consorts are renowned as eight" teaches the eight yoginīs divided into outer and inner. In its provisional meaning, it teaches that, in order to abandon the conceptual grasping at a self found in ordinary people, one should actually practice as the tantra describes. In its definitive meaning, the nature of the eight consorts in the heart is the eight drops facing upward; this is certain.

Then: "Embracing firmly and kissing, yogins honor these. Then, drinking the camphor, one makes the offering and cleansing of the maṇḍala. Then the yogin drinks, and will swiftly attain accomplishment." Here the consorts are brought into the maṇḍala, distinguished as worldly and beyond the world. By the drinking of the awakening mind, the yogins are satisfied, and by performing the cleansing offering of the maṇḍala with it, one attains the common accomplishments. That is the provisional meaning. In the definitive meaning, the eight life-winds travel by the right channel, and by drawing in the nectar of wisdom, the maṇḍala of the body is offered and cleansed. By this one attains accomplishment without fail; that is the instruction of the Tathāgata.

Further: "Giving drink of wine, and eating meat and the rice that grows in water." This is the ritual procedure to be performed beforehand by the master. Likewise, the passage beginning "having made her free of clothing" and ending "play games with her" is meant for the childish who are in the power of desire to understand. As is said in the treatise of Bhulai, one should experience the union of immeasurable flavor, like play with the nails and teeth and the white-clasp and so on.

Then, in the second session: "Having covered the eyes with cloth, bring the disciple there." After the six sessions have passed, the disciple is brought into the maṇḍala of Hevajra, the unbearable one, the source of phenomena by nature, which represents the arising of all bliss. The meaning is that one should also show him his own special deity.

The order is as follows. At night, in a house empty of people, once the master and the others have been distinguished, one gives the empowerment, as it is taught, directly to him. Here the intelligent vajra master avoids the fault of confusion in empowerment, and gives the four empowerments in order. By the words "once the master and the others have been distinguished" the number is implied to be four. For this tantra itself says, "Thus everything is fourfold," and the explanatory tantra of this very text, the Ḍākinī Vajra Tent, says: "The first is the vase empowerment; the second is called the secret; the third is wisdom and gnosis; the fourth is likewise the same."

The master's empowerment, in its provisional meaning, is the vase. In its definitive meaning, only one who is fully perfected in the fourth and the rest is called a master, not one who has merely the vase. Why? Because he does not fit the etymology of "master." Therefore the meaning of the words "distinguished by twos and twos, the vase and the rest end with the fourth; by the distinction of worldly and beyond the world, one should know the empowerment to be complete" is as follows. This should be understood in full from the great root tantras, namely the root tantra in five hundred thousand verses, the one called the Explanation of the Hundred Thousand Tantras, and the one called the Primordial Buddha. Here it is taught only in brief.

"How offering and praise are explained, the good disciple should know beforehand." This means that the intelligent disciple should first honor the guru and praise him with the praises given in the tantra, such as "In your body there is no emptiness of the hollow." Then, "What is taught to him is the end of the supreme first beyond joy, hidden in all the tantras, distinguished by the last of the last." Here, so that the childish may for the moment understand through the earlier stations, that which begins in the waning fortnight and ends in the waxing, hidden in all the tantras, is to be shown to the disciple, the supreme person. That is the meaning.

This is also stated in the Net of Illusion: "Free of attachment, the first is greatly attached." Likewise, in the tantra called the Supreme Primordial Buddha it is said: "Filled with the latent imprints of both sides, unmixed." The meaning is that suchness itself is to be shown in a way that does not contradict any of the tantras.

"Having properly engaged in the vajra offering, the goddess then asked him: What is the instant of that deity? May the great chief explain it." Here Nairātmyā asked the Blessed One: by what instant does one abide in the deity of bliss, and how is it to be realized? And the Blessed One answered in turn:

"There, there is no beginning, middle, or end. It is neither existence nor nirvāṇa. This is itself supreme great bliss, neither self nor other."

The instant of that goddess is existence, that is, passing on from the thirty-two places. It is not nirvāṇa, since it has not passed beyond by any gate. It is also what is meant by "no beginning, end, or middle," and by "neither self nor other"; it is also the instant that is free of defining characteristics.

As it is likewise said in the Tantra of the Equal Union of All Buddhas: "Without beginning or end, peaceful, the chief is without being and without nonbeing; emptiness and compassion are inseparable, and this is called the awakening mind." Therefore the meaning of this vajra word is to be realized and understood in this way.

"From the two waves of enjoyment, with one's own right and left hands, with thumb and ring finger, the yogin now presses." Here the skilled vajra master keeps it secret with secrecy from the disciple who stands in the stream of flowing water on the two sides of his own banner. Likewise, by the power of whatever samādhi one has pressed, it goes by the right and comes by the left; this is the meaning. And likewise, if for some persons wholly bound by conceptual thought the master actually presses with his right and left hands at the right and left of the site of the wheel of complete enjoyment, then wisdom will arise from it. What arises from it? "It is like the joy of a youth, or like the dream of a mute." Here this is distinguished by a difference among three states, and the distinction is that it is different from the realm of expression. For example, a monk who has attained the absorption at the peak of the fourth meditative concentration has a bliss, and what arises is something even more excellent than that; this is the sense. This meaning is also stated in the root tantra called the Supreme First: "Bliss gained through youthful vigor is the great bliss of one's own awareness; likewise, without absorption one cannot speak of bliss." The meaning is to be understood fully.

"The supreme is free of extremes and of joy, in the center." This was shown extensively above. "Empty and not empty, Heruka": the sense is that it will be realized, and the words are to be construed accordingly. Empty is that by which one crosses over; not empty is existence, that is, it does not abide in the maṇḍala of the thirty-two. This is the meaning.

This concludes the fifth chapter, the extensive explanation of the arising of Hevajra, from the Extensive Explanation of the Definitive Meaning of Hevajra.

Having paid homage to the wondrous, marvelous one who has a form in order to tame beings, Vajragarbha writes out the sequence of the "debt." Now, in order to introduce childlike persons, and in order to show an artificial maṇḍala on cloth and the like, in distinction from the maṇḍala as it truly is, the text first says, "Having first embraced the goddess and experienced the bliss of union," and so on. It should be construed thus: the Blessed One, having brought to completion an immeasurable treatise on the dance, taught the rite.

Of these, first, the meaning of the five mudrās taught in the chapter on conduct is to be distinguished as outer and inner and held by those who abide in the yoga of conduct, as was well explained in that very context. In this context of drawing the "debt" painting, in order to show that the master should not be disparaged on account of his own five pledges, and should be served in every respect, the text begins by saying: "First, the five mudrās being distinguished, the lama, the master, and the chosen deity are honored; the wheel is taught for the sake of homage," and on through "the belt itself for the sake of serving the mudrā," and so on. Of the distinction into outer and inner, the outer is just as the words say, as taught in the tantra. The definitive meaning is as stated in the root tantra itself. "The lama, master, and chosen deity" means that, in order to realize that the source of weighty qualities abides in the maṇḍala of the thirty-two, it is marked by the wheel for the sake of reverence. In order to turn back unruly persons who revile that very thing, and so that one does not decline from one's own abode, it is marked by the earrings. In order to strive to keep the life-wind from perishing, it is marked by the necklace. So as not to cut the life of the awakening mind, it is marked by the bracelets. So as not to transgress the maṇḍala of the sixty-four grounds of bliss, it is marked by the belt.

Therefore it is said: "Always seal the body with the mudrās of the five buddhas." In meaning, what is subtle in the five abodes of the life-wind of all embodied beings, that is to be understood as "always seal the body with the mudrās."

Then "having burst into laughter," and so on, through "the painting of Kye's vajra itself, the method of making it, was spoken by great bliss": this is to be connected as the question asked by Nairātmyā about how one draws the painting for the sake of the child. The answer is shown by "Here the painter is one who keeps the pledges, and the practitioner too is one who keeps the pledges; the painting, too, is the drawing of the terrifying," and so on. In this way, the practitioner, the painter, and whoever and whatever engages in the work of the picture are to be those who mostly abide in the path of the inseparability of emptiness and compassion, and who have supreme faith in the way of secret mantra. This is how it is to be construed.

Now the meaning of the limbs is shown. The root text says: "In the skull-vessel of a man, five colors, with a brush of a corpse's hair, one should paint the unsurpassed painted image." The sense is this. The five colors are placed in a skull-vessel of a man, and a brush is made from the hair of a man who has died. The five colors, according to their natural qualities, are set in the skull of that particular man.

Likewise: "In the yoga of the pledge and the blessing, on the fourteenth day of the dark half of the lunar month, in a deserted house, with the mind of a fierce one who has split it open," and so forth. What is meant is this. Whatever objects are needed, one should bless them and put them to use as the lord of the practitioner, the master, instructs. On the fourteenth, the new-moon day of the dark half, one enjoys the feast in a secluded place. The painter and the practitioner himself should be naked, adorned with bone ornaments and smeared with ash, and should paint while eating the impure pledge substances.

Likewise, further: "A woman of good countenance who possesses compassion, who has beauty, youth and good fortune, adorned with flowers, delights the practitioner. One should place one's own consort on the left." That is, the practitioner or the painter places on his left a consort like himself, such as Nairātmyā. Then, abiding in the undivided path, he paints the image for the sake of his aim. This is the definitive meaning according to the Vajra-holder.

This is the sixth chapter, the extensive explanation of painting for the sake of one's aim, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Homage to Kye-vajra, who shows the gross and the subtle directly. The Vajra Heart sets down the extensive explanation through the collection of the sūtra volumes and through the good discipline.

Now, in order to show the stages for writing the volume of Hevajra, the feast gathering taught in the chapter on empowerment, and the stages of the vehicle that children may realize, the text says: "Then the goddess asked him," and so forth. Having satisfied her with the treatise on painting for one's aim, he declared: "I shall explain the volume. Great goddess, of great fortune, listen." The meaning is this. O great goddess, a pledge-holder who writes should write the volume, twelve finger-widths in size, with the hot blood of a great bee and with a pen made from a human bone. In this way, the essence of the sun and the moon, thoroughly purified, is mixed into the blood. The volume is made to measure twelve finger-widths, purified as the twelve links of dependent origination. What is made in this way gives the accomplishment of joy by the power of its natural qualities. That is the definitive point.

"If one without good fortune sees the volume or the painted image, he will have no accomplishment in this life, and in other worlds it will not be his domain." Thus, a volume or a painted cloth accomplished flawlessly through the oral instruction of the master and the blessing of my own power, if shown even once to a person of childish mind who lacks good fortune, causes both kinds of accomplishment to be lost. Therefore the text says: "It should be guarded everywhere with effort."

"To one who has genuine faith, show it only occasionally." This means that one shows it for an instant to a person who has faith and devotion. When offerings, reverence and so forth come first, the showing is not stained by the fault of flaws.

"On the road, in travel, hide the volume in the hair and under the arm." This means that when traveling on roads or to other lands one hides the volume in the hair. The painting itself is put inside a smooth, beautiful, shining case and carried under the left arm.

Now, in order to show the special features of the feast offering, he says: "Having arranged the bhaga and liṅga, repeatedly make the cry 'O!'" and so forth. By its power it destroys plague, white hair, wrinkles and the like, delights ordinary people, and increases radiance with flesh and blood. That is what is to be explained.

Therefore he declares: "Listen to the food of the feast gathering, O wide-eyed goddess. By eating it, all desires and the accomplishments of the practitioner come about." The meaning of this vajra word should be understood in two ways, as definitive and as provisional.

First, for the place of the outer feast food, the text indicates: "A charnel ground, the shelter of a mountain bird, likewise an uninhabited city, or a solitary place, or the shore of the ocean." The meaning is that one begins in a place where no non-Buddhists and the like, nor any wild ordinary people, ever come. Now, in these outer places, eating meat such as that of the bola and drinking liquor, and joining in union with the female organ, cannot bring about the desired accomplishments. If such things were to become the desired accomplishments of those people, then the eighty-four thousand heaps of Dharma would be pointless, and the perfect effort of the yogī would be destroyed. Therefore the teaching that one obtains the desired accomplishments by honoring with a feast of food, drink and so forth is merely a provisional teaching for ordinary people.

As the definitive meaning, relying on some place not stained by any conceptual thought, one is intoxicated by the essence prepared and burned in the fire of life force. Eating to satisfaction and abiding there increases life, and one is given a radiance like that of Vajradhara. That is the definitive meaning.

"This food is the supreme food. For a seat, consider nine corpse forms, or else a tiger skin, or likewise a charnel-ground cloth." Regarding this, one who wishes to perform this feast gathering should seat monks, nuns, householder yogīs, elders, and the rest each in their own separate rows, and should carefully guard against unruly people. Likewise, one invites yoginīs of the families of Gaurī and the rest. One is placed to the left of the chief figure, and the remaining eight are invited to sit in order, in the petal directions and the intermediate directions. For each of them, a human skin one cubit in size, with her own form painted on it, is offered as a seat. For the chief figure, the skin of a tiger, an elephant or a cow is offered as a seat.

Or else, for all of them, one may offer simply human skin alone, or simply tiger skin alone, or simply elephant skin alone, or simply cow skin alone, or simply cloth from the charnel ground alone, offering to each whatever can be found. For this reason it was said at length:

In the center, the form of Hevajra.
Knowing the placement as before,
in the directions and the intermediate directions as well,
then arrange the yoginīs.

In the same way, all the food too should be laid out upon tiger skins and the like, as much as is available. Likewise:

Having eaten there again and again,
there too the mothers are to be honored.

This means that the outer foods and drinks are purified, realized, and transformed, made into nectar, and then eaten, and that, again and again, one realizes bliss with the goddesses through union (mithuna). As for the yoginīs, it is certain that one first purifies them well, in terms of earth, water, fire, wind and so on, as sisters, daughters, female companions, nieces, wives and the like, and then makes offerings to them. In the same way:

Having always honored them well,
the maṇḍala of the gathering will be accomplished.

To honor them well means this: if one honors with the awakening mind that does not turn back, those who are unseen and unknown to outsiders, such as the mother and the sister, then one honors well, and it is certain that the maṇḍala of the gathering will be accomplished. In the same way, the drinking vessels are to be skull fragments of a person, each a single piece, filled with grape wine and the like, and offered.

Offer to the guru the share of the greatest portion.
Having bowed, drink oneself as well.
What is taken with the lotus hand,
with that same hand it is offered.
Bowing deeply again and again,
there the practitioner is to act.

This means that the yoginīs, and the yogins together with the chief, should partake well in this way. All of them, with palms joined at the crown as the preliminary, bow to the master, and having made the offering with the lotus hand, they then partake as they please. In the same way, the cup is to be passed around the entire circle by the lotus hand, and then taken back by that same hand. In terms of the definitive meaning, the drink that is like nectar is passed continuously from the wheel of bliss up to the tip of the banner, and then sent back to its own place, so that one is satisfied by the taking. This is the instruction of the Tathāgata.

This is the seventh chapter, the extensive explanation of writing the book and of the feast-gathering foods, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Then the yoginī asked:

What is the great mudrā like?
Please explain bliss and the rest
through the form of the conventional aspect.

Thus the yoginīs and the rest made their request to the Blessed One, the great bliss, that is, they questioned Hevajra. Here the meaning of what was asked is this: Kye, Blessed One, the intrinsic nature of the great mudrā abides in nothing at all, is inexpressible, without support, without object, and abides nowhere. Since there is no doubt about it, how are those who are covered by the stains of self and what belongs to self to realize the way of it? The Blessed One then spoke again:

Neither very long nor short,
neither black nor white,
like the form of a lotus petal.

and so on. In this way, one should rely on consorts born from the four families, from the twelfth year of age up to the twentieth, and she will become the sign, not otherwise. Their characteristics are these: the body is neither very tall nor too short; neither as deeply dark as eye-salve nor as very white as ash. This concerns the distinctions of complexion, and the extremes of their coloring are to be avoided. What, then, is it like? One should take one with a slightly bluish tinge that stays steady. Likewise, the eyes are like lotus petals, white mixed with red, somewhat long. The breath of the mouth is supremely fragrant, and the blood of the moon-sign has a fragrance like perfume. The lotus of the birth place, from time to time, gives off a scent like sarjarāja resin, and sometimes a scent like a lotus, and from time to time a scent like the utpala and like black agarwood. The wise should recognize these as good fragrances and take such a woman. Likewise, the signs of natural abiding are these: her deportment is gentle and free of coarseness; she listens when others speak, speaks plainly yet slowly; her hair is dark and soft, thick at the partings and long; and in the middle of her forehead there are three lines present by nature. One of this kind is highly praised by the Sugatas, as was also said, "the secret limb." If, applying all effort, one practices with her according to the guru's instruction, one will without doubt attain the accomplishment of the great mudrā. Moreover, as for such a consort, ordinary worldly people who do not know her take her to have the ordinary birth place of a woman. She is not like that, for it is said:

The one with form, born together.

And:

Having found her, accomplishment will come.

This means that if one finds a consort endowed with such perfect qualities and practices relying on her according to the guru's instruction, the accomplishment of the great mudrā will come about. This is the certainty of the Tathāgata. Such a consort is explained as the great mudrā possessing conventional form. That is the meaning.

Then the yoginī Nairātmyā asked: Blessed One, how should aspirations be made? Nairātmyā said this: Kye, Blessed One, how should those who dwell in this great secret of Hevajra make their aspirations? Having thus asked, the Blessed One then spoke again as follows.

May I be born in the family of those who drink the nectar of the pledge, free from madness. May I be born as one who shows the Kye Vajra, keeping the pledge, endowed with compassion and devoted to the guru. May I ring the vajra bell in my hand, teach the profound Dharma, and share and partake of the consorts' seed. May I be born as one who eats it.

This is what was said. One who is intoxicated by the liquor of the bliss that flows is mad; but one who has the bliss of drinking without emission is free of madness. Therefore the meaning is: may I be born into the family of those who drink the nectar of the pledge, free from madness. Never separated from the pledges of the sixteen compassions, may I teach the Dharma to all sentient beings through the expression "Kye" and through the symbol called "vajra." May I have undivided compassion for all sentient beings, and likewise the focus on all sentient beings, the focus on phenomena, and the absence of focus, which is the quality of the waxing moon. Because the cause that brings the state of Vajradhara the Great comes from the vase of the venerable guru's mouth, may I always be devoted to the guru. May I take in hand the vajra and bell, the nature of the path of sun and moon, emptiness and compassion made inseparable and perfectly pure, and may I make them resound. May I always have the practice of reading the scripture of the Kye Vajra itself, which shows that very reality. The recitation of the life-wind through the gates of the vowels and consonants is the reading of the profound Dharma, so may I read such Dharma. And may I eat, from birth to birth, bringing into balance the blissful seed of consorts such as Rūpavajrā and others such as the Mistress of the Expanse of Space. By this, the aspiration is to be made. Such is the meaning.

Then the goddess, pleased, asked these very words again: By what means can the unfortunate beings, hard to tame, be tamed? So says the text. Nairātmyā again asked the Blessed One: Blessed One, in the latter time, when strife flourishes, by what means shall I bring the hard-to-tame, unruly beings into the Kye Vajra? And the Blessed One spoke again:

First give the restoration ceremony. After that, the ten bases of training. Then teach the Vaibhāṣika, and likewise the Sautrāntika. Then the Yogācāra itself, and after that, teach the Madhyamaka.

This is what he said. When bringing hard-to-tame people to the Kye Vajra, one should first teach and train them well in the restoration ceremony. One should cultivate taking up the ten virtues and fully cut off the ten non-virtues. To a person established in that, one should then teach the doctrines of the several philosophical schools. In this way: space, analytical cessation, and non-analytical cessation, these three, are unconditioned, and everything other than them is of the nature of the moment. They are empty of intrinsic nature, and there is no agent at all. By a cognition relying on a sense faculty without images, these accumulated subtle particles come to be known directly. One should teach that this is what the Kashmiri Vaibhāṣikas assert. Likewise, one should teach that the Sautrāntikas hold that the arising of a cognition endowed with an aspect, as the Vaibhāṣikas assert, is not so: appearances are not the domain of the senses, being like a flower in the sky or the son of a barren woman; cessations are like space; the three times have no pursuit; and a form deprived of tangibility that obstructs does not exist either. And likewise, one should teach that the Yogācārins say that a composite substance made of an assembly of subtle particles does not exist; that individual appearance without subtle particles is not apprehended, being like the experience of a dream; and that consciousness, free of the stains of grasped and grasper, like pure crystal, self-aware, exists ultimately. The wise do not accept that consciousness exists ultimately, because it lacks the reasoning of the one and the many in its nature, and so is like a flower in the sky. Thus mind and word are neither existent nor nonexistent, and beyond existence and nonexistence. That suchness, liberated from the four extremes and so forth, is what the great yogic Madhyamikas hold. After thus teaching all the philosophical tenets, if one then brings the person into the Kye Vajra, he will certainly succeed. Such is the meaning.

When the disciple receives it with devotion, there is no doubt that he will succeed in this. So it says, meaning that unfortunate beings are to be led in just this sequence, and not otherwise. Likewise it is said in the Net of Illusion: By three vehicles one emerges; by one vehicle one abides in the fruit.

This concludes the eighth chapter, the extensive explanation of taming, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Kye Vajra.

Having prostrated to the Sovereign Lord, the great king of the enjoyment body, complete body, and emanation, the extensive explanation of the collected mantras will be written by Vajragarbha. Then the characteristics of distinguishing and joining will be rightly explained. So it begins, and so on. If one knows as it is the nature of the mantras of the vowels and consonants, then all wished-for accomplishments, worldly and beyond the world, will be in the palm of one's hand. About this it says: By the mere awareness of whom the practitioner obtains accomplishment, at the root of the navel of the one to be accomplished, one seizes it with the hand of one who keeps the observance.
